S6 EEE 🔌 Power Electronics (EET306)

3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EET306 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Power Electronics

Solid-state energy conversion: Power semiconductor switching devices (SCR, MOSFET, IGBT), line-commutated controlled rectifiers (single-phase & three-phase converters), DC-DC switch-mode buck, boost, and buck-boost choppers, voltage-source (VSI) & current-source (CSI) inverters, sinusoidal pulse width modulation (SPWM), and cycloconverters.

Module 1

Power Semiconductor Devices

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Theory & Analytical ID: PE-Q1

Explain the physical construction and static V-I characteristics of a Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR). Derive the mathematical expression for anode current using the two-transistor analogy. Explain the physical construction and static V-I characteristics of a Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR). Derive the mathematical expression for anode current using the two-transistor analogy. Explain the physical construction and static V-I characteristics of a Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR). Derive the mathematical expression for anode current using the two-transistor analogy.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_A = \frac{\alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)}, \quad I_L \approx (2 \text{ to } 3) I_H$$

SCR 4-layer $p_1-n_1-p_2-n_2$ structure modeled as coupled PNP ($Q_1$) and NPN ($Q_2$) transistors. Forward latching occurs via regenerative positive feedback when loop gain $\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \to 1$. Latching current $I_L$ is minimum turn-on holding value; $I_H$ is drop-out threshold.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_A = \frac{\alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)}, \quad I_L \approx (2 \text{ to } 3) I_H$$

SCR 4-layer $p_1-n_1-p_2-n_2$ structure modeled as coupled PNP ($Q_1$) and NPN ($Q_2$) transistors. Forward latching occurs via regenerative positive feedback when loop gain $\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \to 1$. Latching current $I_L$ is minimum turn-on holding value; $I_H$ is drop-out threshold.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_A = \frac{\alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)}, \quad I_L \approx (2 \text{ to } 3) I_H$$

SCR 4-layer $p_1-n_1-p_2-n_2$ structure modeled as coupled PNP ($Q_1$) and NPN ($Q_2$) transistors. Forward latching occurs via regenerative positive feedback when loop gain $\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \to 1$. Latching current $I_L$ is minimum turn-on holding value; $I_H$ is drop-out threshold.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR) is a four-layer, three-junction ($p_1-n_1-p_2-n_2$) semiconductor switching device possessing three external terminals: Anode ($A$), Cathode ($K$), and Gate ($G$). It is a unidirectional, bistable switch capable of handling high voltages (up to several kilovolts) and large currents (up to several kiloamperes).

### 1. Physical Structure and Operating States The device consists of four alternating semiconductor layers forming three distinct $p-n$ junctions denoted as $J_1$, $J_2$, and $J_3$ from anode to cathode: - **Junction $J_1$:** Formed between outer $p_1$ layer (Anode) and lightly doped inner $n_1$ layer (drift region). - **Junction $J_2$:** Formed between inner $n_1$ layer and moderately doped inner $p_2$ layer (Gate). - **Junction $J_3$:** Formed between inner $p_2$ layer and heavily doped outer $n_2$ layer (Cathode).

The static $V-I$ characteristics of an SCR exhibit three distinct operating regions: 1. **Reverse Blocking Mode ($V_{AK} < 0$, $I_g = 0$):** When the anode is made negative with respect to the cathode, junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ are reverse-biased while junction $J_2$ is forward-biased. The device behaves as two reverse-biased diodes in series. Only a small reverse leakage current flows until the applied voltage reaches the reverse breakdown voltage ($V_{BR}$). Beyond $V_{BR}$, avalanche breakdown occurs at $J_1$ and $J_3$, leading to destructive thermal runaway if current is not externally limited. 2. **Forward Blocking Mode ($V_{AK} > 0$, $I_g = 0$):** When the anode is made positive with respect to the cathode, outer junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ are forward-biased, but the central junction $J_2$ is reverse-biased. A wide depletion region forms across $J_2$, blocking the flow of forward current except for a negligible forward leakage current. The SCR acts as an open switch. If forward voltage is increased to the forward breakover voltage ($V_{BO}$), avalanche breakdown of $J_2$ occurs, causing the device to switch into conduction. 3. **Forward Conduction Mode ($V_{AK} > 0$, $I_g > 0$):** When a positive gate current pulse is applied while $V_{AK} > 0$, junction $J_2$ breaks down at a forward voltage well below $V_{BO}$. All three junctions ($J_1, J_2, J_3$) become forward-biased, and the device transitions into a high-conduction state. The voltage drop across the conducting SCR collapses to a low value ($V_{on} \approx 1.0\text{ to }1.5\text{ V}$), and the forward current is governed entirely by the external load impedance.

``` Anode (A) │ ┌─────┴─────┐ p1 │ Anode │ Junction J1 (Forward biased) ├───────────┤ n1 │ Drift (W) │ Junction J2 (Reverse biased in blocking) G ────┤ Base │ p2 │ Gate │ Junction J3 (Forward biased) ├───────────┤ n2 │ Cathode │ └─────┬─────┘ │ Cathode (K) ```

### 2. Definition of Latching Current and Holding Current - **Latching Current ($I_L$):** The minimum anode current that the SCR must attain during the turn-on process before the gate trigger signal can be safely removed, such that the SCR remains latched in the forward conduction mode. If the anode current does not reach $I_L$, the device reverts to the forward blocking state upon removal of the gate pulse. - **Holding Current ($I_H$):** The minimum anode current below which the SCR drops out of forward conduction and reverts back to the forward blocking state during the turn-off process. - **Relationship:** For any given thyristor, $I_L > I_H$, typically related by: $$I_L \approx 2 \text{ to } 3 \times I_H$$

### 3. Two-Transistor Analogy and Mathematical Derivation The regenerative switching mechanism of an SCR is explained by slicing the four-layer structure vertically into two interconnected complementary transistors: - A $p_1-n_1-p_2$ transistor representing a PNP transistor ($Q_1$). - An $n_1-p_2-n_2$ transistor representing an NPN transistor ($Q_2$).

``` Anode (I_A) │ ┌──┴──┐ │ Q1 │ (PNP) │ ├──┐ I_C1 └──┬──┘ │ I_B1 │ │ │ ┌──▼──┐ ├──┤ Q2 │ (NPN) I_g ──┴─►│ │ └──┬──┘ │ Cathode (I_K = I_A + I_g) ```

From transistor junction theory, the collector current $I_C$ of a bipolar junction transistor is expressed as: $$I_C = \alpha I_E + I_{CBO}$$ where $\alpha$ is the common-base current gain, $I_E$ is the emitter current, and $I_{CBO}$ is the collector-to-base leakage current with emitter open.

For transistor $Q_1$ (PNP): - Emitter current: $I_{E1} = I_A$ - Collector current: $$I_{C1} = \alpha_1 I_A + I_{CBO1}$$

For transistor $Q_2$ (NPN): - Emitter current: $I_{E2} = I_K = I_A + I_g$ - Collector current: $$I_{C2} = \alpha_2 I_K + I_{CBO2} = \alpha_2 (I_A + I_g) + I_{CBO2}$$

From Kirchhoff's Current Law applied at the central junction plane, the total anode current entering the device must equal the sum of the two collector currents: $$I_A = I_{C1} + I_{C2}$$

Substituting the expressions for $I_{C1}$ and $I_{C2}$: $$I_A = \left(\alpha_1 I_A + I_{CBO1}\right) + \left(\alpha_2 (I_A + I_g) + I_{CBO2}\right)$$

Expanding and collecting the terms containing $I_A$: $$I_A = (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2) I_A + \alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}$$ $$I_A \left[1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)\right] = \alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}$$

Solving explicitly for the anode current $I_A$: $$I_A = \frac{\alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)}$$

### 4. Regenerative Latching Mechanism In the forward blocking state without gate current ($I_g = 0$), the current gains $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ are very small ($\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \ll 1$) because of the low emitter currents. Consequently, $I_A$ is limited to the tiny leakage current: $$I_A \approx \frac{I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)} \approx \text{few milliamperes}$$

When a positive gate current $I_g$ is injected into the base of $Q_2$: 1. $I_{C2}$ increases, which directly feeds into the base of $Q_1$ ($I_{B1} = I_{C2}$). 2. Transistor $Q_1$ turns on harder, increasing $I_{C1}$, which feeds back into the base of $Q_2$ ($I_{B2} = I_{C1} + I_g$). 3. The current gains $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ are strong functions of emitter current; as current rises, both $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ increase rapidly. 4. When the loop gain reaches unity: $$\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \to 1$$ 5. The denominator $[1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)] \to 0$, causing $I_A \to \infty$ (limited only by external circuit load resistance). This represents regenerative internal positive feedback, driving both transistors into hard saturation. Once triggered, the internal loop current sustains itself, rendering the gate current $I_g$ completely redundant.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR) is a four-layer, three-junction ($p_1-n_1-p_2-n_2$) semiconductor switching device possessing three external terminals: Anode ($A$), Cathode ($K$), and Gate ($G$). It is a unidirectional, bistable switch capable of handling high voltages (up to several kilovolts) and large currents (up to several kiloamperes).

### 1. Physical Structure and Operating States The device consists of four alternating semiconductor layers forming three distinct $p-n$ junctions denoted as $J_1$, $J_2$, and $J_3$ from anode to cathode: - **Junction $J_1$:** Formed between outer $p_1$ layer (Anode) and lightly doped inner $n_1$ layer (drift region). - **Junction $J_2$:** Formed between inner $n_1$ layer and moderately doped inner $p_2$ layer (Gate). - **Junction $J_3$:** Formed between inner $p_2$ layer and heavily doped outer $n_2$ layer (Cathode).

The device behaves as two reverse-biased diodes in series. Only a small reverse leakage current flows until the applied voltage reaches the reverse breakdown voltage ($V_{BR}$). Beyond $V_{BR}$, avalanche breakdown occurs at $J_1$ and $J_3$, leading to destructive thermal runaway if current is not externally limited. 2. **Forward Blocking Mode ($V_{AK} > 0$, $I_g = 0$):** When the anode is made positive with respect to the cathode, outer junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ are forward-biased, but the central junction $J_2$ is reverse-biased. ``` Anode (A) │ ┌─────┴─────┐ p1 │ Anode │ Junction J1 (Forward biased) ├───────────┤ n1 │ Drift (W) │ Junction J2 (Reverse biased in blocking) G ────┤ Base │ p2 │ Gate │ Junction J3 (Forward biased) ├───────────┤ n2 │ Cathode │ └─────┬─────┘ │ Cathode (K) ```

### 2. Definition of Latching Current and Holding Current - **Latching Current ($I_L$):** The minimum anode current that the SCR must attain during the turn-on process before the gate trigger signal can be safely removed, such that the SCR remains latched in the forward conduction mode. If the anode current does not reach $I_L$, the device reverts to the forward blocking state upon removal of the gate pulse. - **Holding Current ($I_H$):** The minimum anode current below which the SCR drops out of forward conduction and reverts back to the forward blocking state during the turn-off process. - **Relationship:** For any given thyristor, $I_L > I_H$, typically related by: $$I_L \approx 2 \text{ to } 3 \times I_H$$

### 3. Two-Transistor Analogy and Mathematical Derivation The regenerative switching mechanism of an SCR is explained by slicing the four-layer structure vertically into two interconnected complementary transistors: - A $p_1-n_1-p_2$ transistor representing a PNP transistor ($Q_1$). - An $n_1-p_2-n_2$ transistor representing an NPN transistor ($Q_2$).

``` Anode (I_A) │ ┌──┴──┐ │ Q1 │ (PNP) │ ├──┐ I_C1 └──┬──┘ │ I_B1 │ │ │ ┌──▼──┐ ├──┤ Q2 │ (NPN) I_g ──┴─►│ │ └──┬──┘ │ Cathode (I_K = I_A + I_g) ```

From transistor junction theory, the collector current $I_C$ of a bipolar junction transistor is expressed as: $$I_C = \alpha I_E + I_{CBO}$$ where $\alpha$ is the common-base current gain, $I_E$ is the emitter current, and $I_{CBO}$ is the collector-to-base leakage current with emitter open.

For transistor $Q_1$ (PNP): - Emitter current: $I_{E1} = I_A$ - Collector current: $$I_{C1} = \alpha_1 I_A + I_{CBO1}$$

For transistor $Q_2$ (NPN): - Emitter current: $I_{E2} = I_K = I_A + I_g$ - Collector current: $$I_{C2} = \alpha_2 I_K + I_{CBO2} = \alpha_2 (I_A + I_g) + I_{CBO2}$$

From Kirchhoff's Current Law applied at the central junction plane, the total anode current entering the device must equal the sum of the two collector currents: $$I_A = I_{C1} + I_{C2}$$

Substituting the expressions for $I_{C1}$ and $I_{C2}$: $$I_A = \left(\alpha_1 I_A + I_{CBO1}\right) + \left(\alpha_2 (I_A + I_g) + I_{CBO2}\right)$$

Solving explicitly for the anode current $I_A$: $$I_A = \frac{\alpha_2 I_g + I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)}$$

### 4. Regenerative Latching Mechanism In the forward blocking state without gate current ($I_g = 0$), the current gains $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ are very small ($\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \ll 1$) because of the low emitter currents. Consequently, $I_A$ is limited to the tiny leakage current: $$I_A \approx \frac{I_{CBO1} + I_{CBO2}}{1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)} \approx \text{few milliamperes}$$

When a positive gate current $I_g$ is injected into the base of $Q_2$: 1. $I_{C2}$ increases, which directly feeds into the base of $Q_1$ ($I_{B1} = I_{C2}$). 2. Transistor $Q_1$ turns on harder, increasing $I_{C1}$, which feeds back into the base of $Q_2$ ($I_{B2} = I_{C1} + I_g$). 3. The current gains $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ are strong functions of emitter current; as current rises, both $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ increase rapidly. 4. When the loop gain reaches unity: $$\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 \to 1$$ 5. The denominator $[1 - (\alpha_1 + \alpha_2)] \to 0$, causing $I_A \to \infty$ (limited only by external circuit load resistance). This represents regenerative internal positive feedback, driving both transistors into hard saturation. Once triggered, the internal loop current sustains itself, rendering the gate current $I_g$ completely redundant.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 1.1: Physical Structure, Static V-I Characteristics, and Two-Transistor Analogy of Silicon Controlled Rectifier (SCR) — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Descriptive & Analytical ID: PE-Q2

Describe the various turn-on methods for an SCR. Explain the gate characteristics, safe triggering area, and design of pulse transformer isolation gate drive circuits. Describe the various turn-on methods for an SCR. Explain the gate characteristics, safe triggering area, and design of pulse transformer isolation gate drive circuits. Describe the various turn-on methods for an SCR. Explain the gate characteristics, safe triggering area, and design of pulse transformer isolation gate drive circuits.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{gm} = V_g I_g = \text{const}, \quad E_s = V_g + R_g I_g, \quad i_c = C_{j2}\frac{dv}{dt}$$

Turn-on via gate pulse, light (LASCR), thermal, or $dv/dt$. Gate operating point must lie inside preferred triggering window bounded by $V_{g\min}, V_{g\max}, I_{g\min}, I_{g\max}$ and peak gate power hyperbola $P_{gm}$. Pulse transformer / optocoupler provides galvanic isolation.

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_{gm} = V_g I_g = \text{const}, \quad E_s = V_g + R_g I_g, \quad i_c = C_{j2}\frac{dv}{dt}$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To switch an SCR from the forward blocking state to the forward conduction state, the central reverse-biased junction $J_2$ must be caused to break down.

### 1. Classification of SCR Turn-on Methods 1. **Forward Voltage Triggering ($V_{AK} \ge V_{BO}$):** When the forward anode-to-cathode voltage is increased to the forward breakover voltage $V_{BO}$ with gate open ($I_g = 0$), the depletion layer at junction $J_2$ experiences avalanche breakdown. This method is generally avoided in practice because high voltage turn-on causes excessive localized power dissipation and device degradation. 2. **Thermal (Temperature) Triggering:** At high junction temperatures, thermally generated electron-hole pairs inside the depletion layer of $J_2$ increase exponentially. Leakage current doubles roughly every $8\text{ to }10^\circ\text{C}$. This increases $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$, causing regenerative breakdown when $(\alpha_1 + \alpha_2) \to 1$. 3. **$dv/dt$ Triggering:** Junction $J_2$ acts as a dielectric capacitor with junction capacitance $C_{j2}$. When a rapidly rising forward voltage is applied, a displacement current flows through $J_2$: $$i = \frac{dq}{dt} = \frac{d(C_{j2} v)}{dt} = C_{j2} \frac{dv}{dt} + v \frac{dC_{j2}}{dt}$$ If $\frac{dv}{dt}$ is excessively large, the displacement current $i$ acts as an internal gate current, triggering the SCR prematurely. This is an undesirable false turn-on mechanism and requires snubber protection. 4. **Light (Radiation) Triggering (LASCR):** Light-Activated SCRs (LASCR) contain a recessed optical window on junction $J_2$. When incident light photons with energy $h\nu \ge E_g$ strike the silicon lattice, electron-hole pairs are generated near $J_2$, initiating regenerative turn-on. It provides complete electrical isolation and is standard in High-Voltage DC (HVDC) transmission converter valves. 5. **Gate Triggering (Standard Controlled Turn-on):** Applying a positive voltage between gate and cathode injects carriers into the $p_2$ layer, initiating regenerative latching at low forward voltages ($V_{AK} \ll V_{BO}$).

### 2. Gate V-I Characteristics and Safe Triggering Area Due to manufacturing tolerances between different SCR units of the same type, gate characteristics are defined by a spread bounded by maximum and minimum curves:

``` Gate Voltage (V_g) ▲ │ P_gm (Peak Gate Power Hyperbola) V_gmax┼───┐ . . . . . . . . │ │ ┌─────────┐ : │ │ │ Preferred│ : │ │ │ Trigger │ : V_gmin┼───┼─┴─────────┴───:─ Load Line (Slope = -R_g) │ │ Non-trigger : └───┼───┼───────────┼──────► Gate Current (I_g) 0 I_gmin I_gmax ```

To ensure reliable triggering without damaging the gate junction: - $V_{g\min}$ and $I_{g\min}$: Minimum gate voltage and current required to ensure firing of all devices of a given rating at the lowest operating temperature. - $V_{g\max}$ and $I_{g\max}$: Maximum limits to prevent reverse breakdown or junction melting. - $P_{gm}$ (Peak Gate Power Hyperbola): Maximum instantaneous power dissipation curve defined by: $$P_{gm} = V_g \cdot I_g = \text{constant}$$ The operating point must lie strictly within the Preferred Triggering Zone bounded by $V_{g\min}$, $V_{g\max}$, $I_{g\min}$, $I_{g\max}$, and the $P_{gm}$ curve.

The gate circuit equation with source voltage $E_s$ and source resistance $R_g$ is: $$E_s = V_g + R_g I_g \implies V_g = E_s - R_g I_g$$ The load line has a vertical intercept at $E_s$ and a slope of $-R_g$.

### 3. Pulse Triggering and Gate Isolation Circuit Using continuous DC gate signals causes excessive average gate power loss ($P_{g,avg} = V_g I_g$) and thermal heating. Practical converters utilize high-frequency pulse trains ($f_p \approx 10\text{ to }50\text{ kHz}$) modulated over the firing angle window. - **Pulse Transformer:** Provides galvanic high-voltage isolation between the low-voltage microcontroller/DSP logic ($5\text{ V}$) and the high-potential thyristor cathode ($400\text{ to }1000\text{ V}$). It passes short microsecond trigger pulses while blocking DC. - **Optocoupler:** An infrared LED coupled with a phototransistor or phototriac provides electrical isolation exceeding $5\text{ kV}$, eliminating ground loops. - **Diode and Resistor Protection:** A diode in series prevents reverse gate voltage breakdown, while a shunt resistor $R_{gk}$ across gate-cathode drains displacement currents caused by high $dv/dt$, preventing spurious turn-on.

$$t_{on} = t_d + t_r + t_p, \quad t_q = t_{rr} + t_{gr}, \quad t_c > t_q \ (\text{commutation})$$

Turn-on transient comprises delay ($t_d$), rise ($t_r$), and spread ($t_p$) times. Turn-off transient requires sweeping out reverse carriers ($t_{rr}$) and thermal recombination of trapped junction charges ($t_{gr}$). Circuit turn-off time must satisfy $t_c > t_q$ to avoid commutation failure.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The dynamic switching characteristics of an SCR describe the transient transitions between the non-conducting (OFF) and conducting (ON) states. Because of internal junction capacitances and finite charge-carrier transit times, transitions cannot occur instantaneously.

### 1. Dynamic Turn-on Characteristics The turn-on process begins when a gate trigger pulse is applied and is divided into three distinct time intervals: $$t_{on} = t_d + t_r + t_p$$

1. **Delay Time ($t_d$):** The time interval between the instant the gate current reaches $0.1 I_g$ and the instant the anode current rises to $0.1 I_A$ (where $I_A$ is full on-state current). Simultaneously, the anode voltage drops from $V_A$ to $0.9 V_A$. During $t_d$, charge carriers are injected into the gate region, establishing conduction only in a tiny filament near the gate cathode boundary. 2. **Rise Time ($t_r$):** The time required for the anode current to rise from $0.1 I_A$ to $0.9 I_A$, during which the anode-to-cathode voltage falls sharply from $0.9 V_A$ to $0.1 V_A$. - Because high voltage and high current coexist across the device during $t_r$, the instantaneous power dissipation ($p(t) = v_A(t) \cdot i_A(t)$) reaches a tremendous peak. - For an inductive load, $t_r$ is longer because the rate of rise of anode current is limited by the circuit time constant ($L/R$). 3. **Spread Time ($t_p$ or $t_s$):** The time taken for the conduction plasma to spread laterally across the entire active cross-sectional area of the cathode junction. During this interval, the anode current increases from $0.9 I_A$ to full load value $I_A$, and the forward voltage drop falls to its steady-state on-state value: $$V_{on} \approx 1.0 \text{ to } 1.5\text{ V}$$

``` Anode Voltage (v_A) & Current (i_A) ▲ v_A │───────┐ │ V_A │\ │ │ \ │ │ \ v_A drops to V_on │ │ \─────────────────────── ├───────┼───┼───────┼───────────────► Time (t) i_A │ │ │ /────┼─────── I_A (Load current) │ │ │ / │ │ │ _│/ │ └───────┴───┴───────┴───────────────► | t_d | t_r | t_p | |<───── t_on ──────>| ```

### 2. Dynamic Turn-off Characteristics (Commutation) Once an SCR is turned on, reducing gate current to zero does not turn it off. Turn-off requires: 1. Reducing the anode current below the holding current ($I_A < I_H$). 2. Applying a reverse voltage across the anode-to-cathode terminals for a sufficient duration to allow trapped excess charge carriers to be removed and recombine.

The internal device turn-off time ($t_q$) consists of two phases: $$t_q = t_{rr} + t_{gr}$$

``` Anode Current (i_A) ▲ I_A ┼───────────┐ │ │\ │ │ \ (di/dt) 0 ┼───────────┼──\───────────────┼────────► Time (t) │ │ \ /│ -I_RR┼ - - - - - ┼ - -\ - - - - - / │ │ │ \_________/ │ │ |<─── t_rr ────>|<─ t_gr ─>| │ |<─────────── t_q ────────>| │ |<──────────── t_c ──────────────>| (t_c > t_q) ▼ ```

1. **Reverse Recovery Time ($t_{rr}$):** When the anode current falls to zero at a rate $di/dt$ determined by external circuit parameters, it continues to flow in the reverse direction due to the sweeping out of stored holes and electrons from outer junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$. The reverse current reaches a peak value $I_{RR}$ (Reverse Recovery Current) and then decays rapidly to zero. At the end of $t_{rr}$, junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ have regained their reverse blocking capability. The reverse recovery charge $Q_{rr}$ is: $$Q_{rr} = \frac{1}{2} I_{RR} \cdot t_{rr} \implies I_{RR} = \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right) t_{rr} \implies t_{rr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 Q_{rr}}{di/dt}}$$ 2. **Gate Recovery Time ($t_{gr}$):** Even after $J_1$ and $J_3$ recover, inner junction $J_2$ remains flooded with trapped minority carriers. These carriers cannot be swept out by external reverse voltage; they must decay solely via internal thermal carrier recombination. The time required for recombination to reduce carrier density below the critical threshold is the gate recovery time $t_{gr}$.

### 3. Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$) and Commutation Failure - **Device Turn-off Time ($t_q$):** The intrinsic physical time required by the SCR to regain forward blocking capability after anode current has ceased. Standard converter-grade thyristors have $t_q \approx 50\text{ to }100\ \mu\text{s}$, while inverter-grade fast thyristors have $t_q \approx 3\text{ to }20\ \mu\text{s}$. - **Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$):** The time duration for which the external power circuit actually applies a reverse bias voltage across the SCR: $$t_c = \frac{\pi - \alpha}{\omega} \quad \text{(in line-commutated converters)}$$ - **Mandatory Commutation Condition:** To guarantee successful commutation and prevent catastrophic short-circuits: $$t_c > t_q$$ Typically, a safety design factor of $1.5 \text{ to } 2$ is enforced ($t_c \ge 1.5 t_q$). If forward voltage is reapplied when $t_c < t_q$, the residual trapped carriers at junction $J_2$ will immediately re-trigger the thyristor into forward conduction without any gate pulse, causing **commutation failure**.

The dynamic switching characteristics of an SCR describe the transient transitions between the non-conducting (OFF) and conducting (ON) states. Because of internal junction capacitances and finite charge-carrier transit times, transitions cannot occur instantaneously.

### 1. Dynamic Turn-on Characteristics The turn-on process begins when a gate trigger pulse is applied and is divided into three distinct time intervals: $$t_{on} = t_d + t_r + t_p$$

Simultaneously, the anode voltage drops from $V_A$ to $0.9 V_A$. During $t_d$, charge carriers are injected into the gate region, establishing conduction only in a tiny filament near the gate cathode boundary. 2. **Rise Time ($t_r$):** The time required for the anode current to rise from $0.1 I_A$ to $0.9 I_A$, during which the anode-to-cathode voltage falls sharply from $0.9 V_A$ to $0.1 V_A$. - Because high voltage and high current coexist across the device during $t_r$, the instantaneous power dissipation ($p(t) = v_A(t) \cdot i_A(t)$) reaches a tremendous peak. - For an inductive load, $t_r$ is longer because the rate of rise of anode current is limited by the circuit time constant ($L/R$). 3. **Spread Time ($t_p$ or $t_s$):** The time taken for the conduction plasma to spread laterally across the entire active cross-sectional area of the cathode junction. ``` Anode Voltage (v_A) & Current (i_A) ▲ v_A │───────┐ │ V_A │\ │ │ \ │ │ \ v_A drops to V_on │ │ \─────────────────────── ├───────┼───┼───────┼───────────────► Time (t) i_A │ │ │ /────┼─────── I_A (Load current) │ │ │ / │ │ │ _│/ │ └───────┴───┴───────┴───────────────► | t_d | t_r | t_p | |<───── t_on ──────>| ```

### 2. Dynamic Turn-off Characteristics (Commutation) Once an SCR is turned on, reducing gate current to zero does not turn it off. Turn-off requires: 1. Reducing the anode current below the holding current ($I_A < I_H$). 2. Applying a reverse voltage across the anode-to-cathode terminals for a sufficient duration to allow trapped excess charge carriers to be removed and recombine.

The internal device turn-off time ($t_q$) consists of two phases: $$t_q = t_{rr} + t_{gr}$$

``` Anode Current (i_A) ▲ I_A ┼───────────┐ │ │\ │ │ \ (di/dt) 0 ┼───────────┼──\───────────────┼────────► Time (t) │ │ \ /│ -I_RR┼ - - - - - ┼ - -\ - - - - - / │ │ │ \_________/ │ │ |<─── t_rr ────>|<─ t_gr ─>| │ |<─────────── t_q ────────>| │ |<──────────── t_c ──────────────>| (t_c > t_q) ▼ ```

1. **Reverse Recovery Time ($t_{rr}$):** When the anode current falls to zero at a rate $di/dt$ determined by external circuit parameters, it continues to flow in the reverse direction due to the sweeping out of stored holes and electrons from outer junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$. The reverse current reaches a peak value $I_{RR}$ (Reverse Recovery Current) and then decays rapidly to zero. At the end of $t_{rr}$, junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ have regained their reverse blocking capability. The reverse recovery charge $Q_{rr}$ is: $$Q_{rr} = \frac{1}{2} I_{RR} \cdot t_{rr} \implies I_{RR} = \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right) t_{rr} \implies t_{rr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 Q_{rr}}{di/dt}}$$ 2. **Gate Recovery Time ($t_{gr}$):** Even after $J_1$ and $J_3$ recover, inner junction $J_2$ remains flooded with trapped minority carriers. These carriers cannot be swept out by external reverse voltage; they must decay solely via internal thermal carrier recombination. The time required for recombination to reduce carrier density below the critical threshold is the gate recovery time $t_{gr}$.

### 3. Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$) and Commutation Failure - **Device Turn-off Time ($t_q$):** The intrinsic physical time required by the SCR to regain forward blocking capability after anode current has ceased. Standard converter-grade thyristors have $t_q \approx 50\text{ to }100\ \mu\text{s}$, while inverter-grade fast thyristors have $t_q \approx 3\text{ to }20\ \mu\text{s}$. - **Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$):** The time duration for which the external power circuit actually applies a reverse bias voltage across the SCR: $$t_c = \frac{\pi - \alpha}{\omega} \quad \text{(in line-commutated converters)}$$ - **Mandatory Commutation Condition:** To guarantee successful commutation and prevent catastrophic short-circuits: $$t_c > t_q$$ Typically, a safety design factor of $1.5 \text{ to } 2$ is enforced ($t_c \ge 1.5 t_q$). If forward voltage is reapplied when $t_c < t_q$, the residual trapped carriers at junction $J_2$ will immediately re-trigger the thyristor into forward conduction without any gate pulse, causing **commutation failure**.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The dynamic switching characteristics of an SCR describe the transient transitions between the non-conducting (OFF) and conducting (ON) states. Because of internal junction capacitances and finite charge-carrier transit times, transitions cannot occur instantaneously.

### 1. Dynamic Turn-on Characteristics The turn-on process begins when a gate trigger pulse is applied and is divided into three distinct time intervals: $$t_{on} = t_d + t_r + t_p$$

1. **Delay Time ($t_d$):** The time interval between the instant the gate current reaches $0.1 I_g$ and the instant the anode current rises to $0.1 I_A$ (where $I_A$ is full on-state current). Simultaneously, the anode voltage drops from $V_A$ to $0.9 V_A$. During $t_d$, charge carriers are injected into the gate region, establishing conduction only in a tiny filament near the gate cathode boundary. 2. **Rise Time ($t_r$):** The time required for the anode current to rise from $0.1 I_A$ to $0.9 I_A$, during which the anode-to-cathode voltage falls sharply from $0.9 V_A$ to $0.1 V_A$. - Because high voltage and high current coexist across the device during $t_r$, the instantaneous power dissipation ($p(t) = v_A(t) \cdot i_A(t)$) reaches a tremendous peak. - For an inductive load, $t_r$ is longer because the rate of rise of anode current is limited by the circuit time constant ($L/R$). 3. **Spread Time ($t_p$ or $t_s$):** The time taken for the conduction plasma to spread laterally across the entire active cross-sectional area of the cathode junction. During this interval, the anode current increases from $0.9 I_A$ to full load value $I_A$, and the forward voltage drop falls to its steady-state on-state value: $$V_{on} \approx 1.0 \text{ to } 1.5\text{ V}$$

``` Anode Voltage (v_A) & Current (i_A) ▲ v_A │───────┐ │ V_A │\ │ │ \ │ │ \ v_A drops to V_on │ │ \─────────────────────── ├───────┼───┼───────┼───────────────► Time (t) i_A │ │ │ /────┼─────── I_A (Load current) │ │ │ / │ │ │ _│/ │ └───────┴───┴───────┴───────────────► | t_d | t_r | t_p | |<───── t_on ──────>| ```

### 2. Dynamic Turn-off Characteristics (Commutation) Once an SCR is turned on, reducing gate current to zero does not turn it off. Turn-off requires: 1. Reducing the anode current below the holding current ($I_A < I_H$). 2. Applying a reverse voltage across the anode-to-cathode terminals for a sufficient duration to allow trapped excess charge carriers to be removed and recombine.

The internal device turn-off time ($t_q$) consists of two phases: $$t_q = t_{rr} + t_{gr}$$

``` Anode Current (i_A) ▲ I_A ┼───────────┐ │ │\ │ │ \ (di/dt) 0 ┼───────────┼──\───────────────┼────────► Time (t) │ │ \ /│ -I_RR┼ - - - - - ┼ - -\ - - - - - / │ │ │ \_________/ │ │ |<─── t_rr ────>|<─ t_gr ─>| │ |<─────────── t_q ────────>| │ |<──────────── t_c ──────────────>| (t_c > t_q) ▼ ```

1. **Reverse Recovery Time ($t_{rr}$):** When the anode current falls to zero at a rate $di/dt$ determined by external circuit parameters, it continues to flow in the reverse direction due to the sweeping out of stored holes and electrons from outer junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$. The reverse current reaches a peak value $I_{RR}$ (Reverse Recovery Current) and then decays rapidly to zero. At the end of $t_{rr}$, junctions $J_1$ and $J_3$ have regained their reverse blocking capability. The reverse recovery charge $Q_{rr}$ is: $$Q_{rr} = \frac{1}{2} I_{RR} \cdot t_{rr} \implies I_{RR} = \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right) t_{rr} \implies t_{rr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 Q_{rr}}{di/dt}}$$ 2. **Gate Recovery Time ($t_{gr}$):** Even after $J_1$ and $J_3$ recover, inner junction $J_2$ remains flooded with trapped minority carriers. These carriers cannot be swept out by external reverse voltage; they must decay solely via internal thermal carrier recombination. The time required for recombination to reduce carrier density below the critical threshold is the gate recovery time $t_{gr}$.

### 3. Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$) and Commutation Failure - **Device Turn-off Time ($t_q$):** The intrinsic physical time required by the SCR to regain forward blocking capability after anode current has ceased. Standard converter-grade thyristors have $t_q \approx 50\text{ to }100\ \mu\text{s}$, while inverter-grade fast thyristors have $t_q \approx 3\text{ to }20\ \mu\text{s}$. - **Circuit Turn-off Time ($t_c$):** The time duration for which the external power circuit actually applies a reverse bias voltage across the SCR: $$t_c = \frac{\pi - \alpha}{\omega} \quad \text{(in line-commutated converters)}$$ - **Mandatory Commutation Condition:** To guarantee successful commutation and prevent catastrophic short-circuits: $$t_c > t_q$$ Typically, a safety design factor of $1.5 \text{ to } 2$ is enforced ($t_c \ge 1.5 t_q$). If forward voltage is reapplied when $t_c < t_q$, the residual trapped carriers at junction $J_2$ will immediately re-trigger the thyristor into forward conduction without any gate pulse, causing **commutation failure**.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 1.3: Dynamic Transient Switching Waveforms of Anode Voltage and Current During Turn-On and Turn-Off of an SCR — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Design & Derivation ID: PE-Q4

Explain the need for di/dt and dv/dt protection for an SCR. Derive the design equations for a series inductor and an RC snubber circuit with a step-by-step numerical example. Explain the need for di/dt and dv/dt protection for an SCR. Derive the design equations for a series inductor and an RC snubber circuit with a step-by-step numerical example. Explain the need for di/dt and dv/dt protection for an SCR. Derive the design equations for a series inductor and an RC snubber circuit with a step-by-step numerical example.

$$L_s \ge \frac{V_s}{(di/dt)_{\max}}, \quad C_s = \frac{V_s^2}{L_s (dv/dt)_{\max}^2}, \quad R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}}, \quad \zeta = \frac{R_s}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C_s}{L_s}}$$

Series inductor $L_s$ suppresses localized gate hot-spot burning from high $di/dt$. Parallel RC snubber dampens transient $dv/dt$ displacement currents across junction $J_2$. Snubber resistor $R_s$ limits discharge current when SCR fires.

$$L_s \ge \frac{V_s}{(di/dt)_{\max}}, \quad C_s = \frac{V_s^2}{L_s (dv/dt)_{\max}^2}, \quad R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}}, \quad \zeta = \frac{R_s}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C_s}{L_s}}$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Power thyristors must be protected against catastrophic electrical stresses: high rates of current rise ($di/dt$), high rates of voltage rise ($dv/dt$), overvoltages, and overcurrents.

### 1. $di/dt$ Protection using Series Inductor ($L_s$) - **Problem:** During the initial turn-on interval ($t_d$ and $t_r$), conduction begins over a tiny localized area near the gate cathode contact. If the anode current rises at an excessively high rate ($di/dt$), current density in this restricted area becomes astronomically high. Localized power dissipation creates microscopic "hot spots" where silicon temperature exceeds $300^\circ\text{C}$, causing permanent localized thermal puncture. - **Remedy:** A small inductor $L_s$ is connected in direct series with the anode. The inductor opposes instantaneous changes in current ($v_L = L \frac{di}{dt}$), limiting the rate of rise: $$\left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\max} \le \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\text{rated}}$$ - **Design Formula:** For a supply voltage $V_s$, the minimum value of series inductance required is: $$L_s \ge \frac{V_s}{(di/dt)_{\max}}$$

### 2. $dv/dt$ Protection using Parallel RC Snubber Circuit - **Problem:** When an SCR is in forward blocking, junction $J_2$ acts as a capacitor with depletion capacitance $C_{j2}$. A steep step voltage or voltage transient causes a capacitive displacement current: $$i_c = C_{j2} \frac{dv}{dt}$$ If $i_c$ exceeds the gate threshold current $I_{g\min}$, the SCR turns on unintentionally without a gate command. - **Remedy:** A snubber circuit comprising a series combination of a non-inductive resistor $R_s$ and capacitor $C_s$ is connected in parallel with the SCR.

``` + ─────────o───[ L_s ]───┬───────────────o + │ ┌─────┴─────┐ │ │ │ [R_s] │ │ │ ▼ SCR │ [Load] [C_s] │ │ │ │ │ └─────┬─────┘ │ - ───────────────────────┴───────────────o - ```

### 3. Mathematical Derivation of RC Snubber Equations When supply voltage $V_s$ is suddenly switched across the uncharged circuit with the SCR in OFF state, the equivalent circuit is a series $R-L-C$ circuit formed by source inductance $L_s$, snubber resistance $R_s$, and snubber capacitance $C_s$.

Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law: $$V_s = L_s \frac{di}{dt} + R_s i + v_c(t)$$ Since the loop current is $i(t) = C_s \frac{dv_c}{dt}$: $$L_s C_s \frac{d^2 v_c}{dt^2} + R_s C_s \frac{dv_c}{dt} + v_c = V_s$$

Dividing by $L_s C_s$: $$\frac{d^2 v_c}{dt^2} + \frac{R_s}{L_s}\frac{dv_c}{dt} + \frac{1}{L_s C_s} v_c = \frac{V_s}{L_s C_s}$$

This represents a standard second-order system characterized by: - Undamped natural frequency: $\omega_n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{L_s C_s}}$ - Damping ratio: $\zeta = \frac{R_s}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C_s}{L_s}}$

The voltage appearing across the SCR is the terminal voltage across the snubber branch: $$v_{AK}(t) = v_c(t) + R_s i(t)$$

At the initial instant of switching ($t = 0^+$), capacitor voltage is zero ($v_c(0^+) = 0$) and current is zero ($i(0^+) = 0$). The rate of change of voltage across the SCR is maximized: $$\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max} \approx \frac{V_s}{L_s} R_s = 2 \zeta \omega_n V_s$$

For an optimally damped response to avoid severe voltage overshoots ($\zeta \approx 0.65\text{ to }0.707$): $$\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max} = 0.563 \omega_n V_s = 0.563 \frac{V_s}{\sqrt{L_s C_s}}$$

Squaring both sides and solving for $C_s$: $$C_s = \frac{(0.563)^2 V_s^2}{L_s \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max}^2} \approx \frac{V_s^2}{L_s \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max}^2}$$

### 4. Snubber Resistor Sizing and Discharge Current Limit When the SCR is triggered into conduction, the snubber capacitor $C_s$ (charged up to $V_s$) discharges locally through $R_s$ and the internal conducting channel of the SCR. The peak discharge current at $t = 0^+$ is: $$I_{dis,\max} = \frac{V_s}{R_s}$$

To ensure this discharge current does not exceed the maximum allowable repetitive surge current rating of the SCR ($I_{T\max}$): $$R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}}$$

Simultaneously, the rate of discharge current rise must not exceed device $(di/dt)_{\max}$: $$\left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\text{discharge}} = \frac{V_s}{R_s C_s} \le \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\max}$$

### 5. Step-by-Step Numerical Example **Given Specifications:** - Supply DC Voltage: $V_s = 400\text{ V}$ - Maximum allowable $(di/dt)_{\max} = 40\text{ A}/\mu\text{s} = 40 \times 10^6\text{ A/s}$ - Maximum allowable $(dv/dt)_{\max} = 200\text{ V}/\mu\text{s} = 200 \times 10^6\text{ V/s}$ - Peak allowable discharge current: $I_{T\max} = 100\text{ A}$

**Step 1: Calculate series inductance $L_s$:** $$L_s \ge \frac{V_s}{(di/dt)_{\max}} = \frac{400}{40 \times 10^6} = 10 \times 10^{-6}\text{ H} = 10\ \mu\text{H}$$

**Step 2: Calculate snubber resistance $R_s$ from discharge limit:** $$R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}} = \frac{400}{100} = 4\ \Omega$$

**Step 3: Calculate snubber capacitance $C_s$:** $$C_s = \frac{V_s^2}{L_s \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max}^2} = \frac{(400)^2}{(10 \times 10^{-6}) \times (200 \times 10^6)^2} = \frac{160000}{10^{-5} \times 4 \times 10^{16}} = \frac{160000}{4 \times 10^{11}} = 0.4\ \mu\text{F}$$

**Step 4: Verify damping ratio $\zeta$:** $$\zeta = \frac{R_s}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C_s}{L_s}} = \frac{4}{2}\sqrt{\frac{0.4 \times 10^{-6}}{10 \times 10^{-6}}} = 2 \times \sqrt{0.04} = 2 \times 0.2 = 0.4$$ To raise $\zeta$ toward optimal $0.65$, $R_s$ is selected as $R_s = 6.5\ \Omega$, safely above the minimum $4\ \Omega$ constraint.

### 3. Mathematical Derivation of RC Snubber Equations When supply voltage $V_s$ is suddenly switched across the uncharged circuit with the SCR in OFF state, the equivalent circuit is a series $R-L-C$ circuit formed by source inductance $L_s$, snubber resistance $R_s$, and snubber capacitance $C_s$.

Dividing by $L_s C_s$: $$\frac{d^2 v_c}{dt^2} + \frac{R_s}{L_s}\frac{dv_c}{dt} + \frac{1}{L_s C_s} v_c = \frac{V_s}{L_s C_s}$$

### 4. Snubber Resistor Sizing and Discharge Current Limit When the SCR is triggered into conduction, the snubber capacitor $C_s$ (charged up to $V_s$) discharges locally through $R_s$ and the internal conducting channel of the SCR. The peak discharge current at $t = 0^+$ is: $$I_{dis,\max} = \frac{V_s}{R_s}$$

To ensure this discharge current does not exceed the maximum allowable repetitive surge current rating of the SCR ($I_{T\max}$): $$R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}}$$

Simultaneously, the rate of discharge current rise must not exceed device $(di/dt)_{\max}$: $$\left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\text{discharge}} = \frac{V_s}{R_s C_s} \le \left(\frac{di}{dt}\right)_{\max}$$

### 5. Step-by-Step Numerical Example **Given Specifications:** - Supply DC Voltage: $V_s = 400\text{ V}$ - Maximum allowable $(di/dt)_{\max} = 40\text{ A}/\mu\text{s} = 40 \times 10^6\text{ A/s}$ - Maximum allowable $(dv/dt)_{\max} = 200\text{ V}/\mu\text{s} = 200 \times 10^6\text{ V/s}$ - Peak allowable discharge current: $I_{T\max} = 100\text{ A}$

**Step 1: Calculate series inductance $L_s$:** $$L_s \ge \frac{V_s}{(di/dt)_{\max}} = \frac{400}{40 \times 10^6} = 10 \times 10^{-6}\text{ H} = 10\ \mu\text{H}$$

**Step 2: Calculate snubber resistance $R_s$ from discharge limit:** $$R_s \ge \frac{V_s}{I_{T\max}} = \frac{400}{100} = 4\ \Omega$$

**Step 3: Calculate snubber capacitance $C_s$:** $$C_s = \frac{V_s^2}{L_s \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)_{\max}^2} = \frac{(400)^2}{(10 \times 10^{-6}) \times (200 \times 10^6)^2} = \frac{160000}{10^{-5} \times 4 \times 10^{16}} = \frac{160000}{4 \times 10^{11}} = 0.4\ \mu\text{F}$$

**Step 4: Verify damping ratio $\zeta$:** $$\zeta = \frac{R_s}{2}\sqrt{\frac{C_s}{L_s}} = \frac{4}{2}\sqrt{\frac{0.4 \times 10^{-6}}{10 \times 10^{-6}}} = 2 \times \sqrt{0.04} = 2 \times 0.2 = 0.4$$ To raise $\zeta$ toward optimal $0.65$, $R_s$ is selected as $R_s = 6.5\ \Omega$, safely above the minimum $4\ \Omega$ constraint.

### 5. Step-by-Step Numerical Example **Given Specifications:** - Supply DC Voltage: $V_s = 400\text{ V}$ - Maximum allowable $(di/dt)_{\max} = 40\text{ A}/\mu\text{s} = 40 \times 10^6\text{ A/s}$ - Maximum allowable $(dv/dt)_{\max} = 200\text{ V}/\mu\text{s} = 200 \times 10^6\text{ V/s}$ - Peak allowable discharge current: $I_{T\max} = 100\text{ A}$

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Comparative Analysis & Device Physics ID: PE-Q5

Compare the operating principles, switching speeds, conduction losses, and Safe Operating Areas (SOA) of TRIAC, GTO, Power MOSFET, and IGBT. Compare the operating principles, switching speeds, conduction losses, and Safe Operating Areas (SOA) of TRIAC, GTO, Power MOSFET, and IGBT. Compare the operating principles, switching speeds, conduction losses, and Safe Operating Areas (SOA) of TRIAC, GTO, Power MOSFET, and IGBT.

$$\beta_{off} = \frac{I_A}{I_{GR}} \approx 3-5 \ (\text{GTO}), \quad R_{DS(on)} \propto V_{BR}^{2.5} \ (\text{MOSFET}), \quad V_{CE(sat)} \approx 1.8-2.5\text{ V} \ (\text{IGBT})$$

TRIAC: Bidirectional AC line switch (4 quadrants).
GTO: Turn-off via large reverse gate current.
MOSFET: Unipolar majority-carrier switch for MHz frequencies ($<600\text{ V}$).
IGBT: Hybrid MOS-input BJT-output with conductivity modulation for medium/high power.

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\beta_{off} = \frac{I_A}{I_{GR}} \approx 3-5 \ (\text{GTO}), \quad R_{DS(on)} \propto V_{BR}^{2.5} \ (\text{MOSFET}), \quad V_{CE(sat)} \approx 1.8-2.5\text{ V} \ (\text{IGBT})$$

TRIAC: Bidirectional AC line switch (4 quadrants).
GTO: Turn-off via large reverse gate current.
MOSFET: Unipolar majority-carrier switch for MHz frequencies ($<600\text{ V}$).
IGBT: Hybrid MOS-input BJT-output with conductivity modulation for medium/high power.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Modern power electronic converters utilize diverse power semiconductor switches depending on the operating power level, voltage blocking requirement, switching frequency, and gate control complexity.

### 1. Device Physics and Operating Principles

#### A. TRIAC (Triode for Alternating Current) - **Physics:** A bidirectional 5-layer thyristor equivalent to two SCRs connected in inverse-parallel with a single common gate terminal. - **Conduction:** Conducts current in either direction between Main Terminals $MT_1$ and $MT_2$. - **Triggering Modes:** Operates in 4 quadrants depending on polarities of $V_{MT2-MT1}$ and $V_{G-MT1}$: - Mode $I^+$ ($V_{MT2} > 0, V_G > 0$): Highly sensitive turn-on. - Mode $I^-$ ($V_{MT2} > 0, V_G < 0$): Moderate sensitivity. - Mode $III^-$ ($V_{MT2} < 0, V_G < 0$): Highly sensitive turn-on. - Mode $III^+$ ($V_{MT2} < 0, V_G > 0$): Least sensitive (avoided in practice). - **Limitation:** Poor commutating $dv/dt$ capability ($dv/dt_c \approx 10\text{ to }20\text{ V}/\mu\text{s}$); when current falls to zero at AC zero crossing, stored charge can cause re-triggering. Limited to low-power $50\text{ Hz}$ AC line regulators (light dimmers, fan speed controllers).

#### B. GTO (Gate Turn-Off Thyristor) - **Physics:** A 4-layer $p-n-p-n$ thyristor designed with an interdigitated multi-finger cathode-gate geometry to minimize lateral base resistance. - **Turn-off Mechanism:** Can be turned off by extracting a large negative gate current pulse ($I_{GR}$). The negative gate pulse extracts stored carriers from the $p$-base, pinching the anode current into narrow filaments until total carrier starvation extinguishes conduction. - **Turn-off Gain ($\beta_{off}$):** $$\beta_{off} = \frac{I_A}{I_{GR}} \approx 3 \text{ to } 5$$ Because $\beta_{off}$ is very low, turning off an anode current of $1000\text{ A}$ requires an instantaneous reverse gate pulse of $200\text{ to }300\text{ A}$, necessitating complex, bulky gate-drive circuitry. - **Application:** High-power traction drives and HVDC systems ($>4\text{ kV}, >3\text{ kA}$) where high conduction capability is paramount.

#### C. Power MOSFET (Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistor) - **Physics:** A unipolar, majority-carrier, voltage-controlled device with a vertical planar (DMOS) or trench architecture. - **Switching Speed:** Because conduction occurs exclusively via majority carriers (electrons in $n$-channel), there is zero minority-carrier storage delay. Switching times are extremely short ($10\text{ to }50\text{ ns}$), permitting switching frequencies up to several megahertz ($>1\text{ MHz}$). - **Input Impedance:** The insulated $SiO_2$ gate provides nearly infinite input DC resistance ($>10^9\ \Omega$); gate drive requires energy only to charge and discharge gate capacitances ($C_{iss} = C_{gs} + C_{gd}$). - **On-State Resistance ($R_{DS(on)}$):** Exhibits a positive temperature coefficient ($dR_{DS(on)}/dT > 0$), making MOSFETs naturally self-balancing when operated in parallel without thermal runaway. - **Limitation:** On-state resistance increases steeply with breakdown voltage ($R_{DS(on)} \propto V_{BR}^{2.5}$). Hence, conduction losses become excessive at ratings above $600\text{ V}$.

#### D. IGBT (Insulated Gate Bipolar Transistor) - **Physics:** A monolithic hybrid power device combining the high-impedance MOS gate structure of a MOSFET with the high-current bipolar output characteristic of a wide-base BJT. - **Conductivity Modulation:** An added $p^+$ substrate injects holes into the lightly doped $n^-$ drift region during conduction. This massive injection floods the drift region with electron-hole plasma, dropping its effective resistance by orders of magnitude (conductivity modulation). - **Conduction Voltage:** On-state saturation voltage remains low and nearly constant ($V_{CE(sat)} \approx 1.5\text{ to }2.5\text{ V}$) even for devices rated up to $6.5\text{ kV}$ and $1500\text{ A}$. - **Turn-Off Tail Current:** During turn-off, the MOS channel opens immediately, but minority carriers stored in the wide $n^-$ drift base can only decay by recombination, causing a persistent "collector current tail" that increases turn-off switching losses.

``` MOSFET Equivalent IGBT Equivalent D (Drain) C (Collector) │ │ ┌───┴───┐ ┌────┴────┐ │ MOSFET│ │ PNP │ │ │ │ BJT │ G ───┤ │ G ───┤ │ └───┬───┘ └────┬────┘ │ MOSFET In│ S (Source) E (Emitter) ```

### 2. Comprehensive Comparative Matrix

| Parameter / Feature | TRIAC | GTO | Power MOSFET | IGBT | | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Control Type** | Current controlled (Pulse) | Current controlled (Bi-directional) | Voltage controlled ($V_{GS}$) | Voltage controlled ($V_{GE}$) | | **Carrier Type** | Bipolar (Minority) | Bipolar (Minority) | Unipolar (Majority only) | Hybrid (Majority + Minority) | | **Voltage Rating** | Medium ($<1.2\text{ kV}$) | Very High ($>6\text{ kV}$) | Low/Medium ($<1000\text{ V}$) | High ($600\text{ V} - 6.5\text{ kV}$) | | **Current Rating** | Low/Medium ($<100\text{ A}$) | Very High ($>3000\text{ A}$) | Medium ($<200\text{ A}$) | High ($>1500\text{ A}$) | | **Switching Frequency** | Very Low ($<400\text{ Hz}$) | Low ($<2\text{ kHz}$) | Very High ($100\text{ kHz} - 2\text{ MHz}$) | Medium/High ($10\text{ kHz} - 100\text{ kHz}$) | | **On-State Loss** | Low ($V_{on} \approx 1.5\text{ V}$) | Low ($V_{on} \approx 2\text{ to }3\text{ V}$) | High at high $V$ ($I^2 R_{DS(on)}$) | Low ($V_{CE(sat)} \approx 1.8\text{ to }2.5\text{ V}$) | | **Switching Loss** | Low at $50\text{ Hz}$ | High (Turn-off tail) | Negligible (Fast edges) | Moderate (Tail current) | | **Gate Drive Power** | Low (Short pulses) | Very High ($\beta_{off} \approx 3-5$) | Minimal (Capacitive only) | Minimal (Capacitive only) | | **Safe Operating Area** | Limited by $dv/dt$ | Moderate (Needs snubber) | Excellent (No 2nd breakdown)| Excellent (Wide FBSOA/RBSOA) | | **Paralleling Ease** | Poor | Poor | Excellent (Positive temp coeff)| Very Good (Modern planar) | | **Primary Applications**| AC regulators, dimmers | Traction, heavy MW drives | SMPS, RF, EV DC-DC | EV inverters, VFD, Renewables |

### 3. Safe Operating Area (SOA) Considerations - **Power MOSFET SOA:** Strictly bounded by thermal dissipation ($P_D = V_{DS} I_D$) and maximum pulsed drain current. Because MOSFETs have a positive temperature coefficient of resistance, hot spots do not form, rendering them immune to second breakdown. - **IGBT SOA:** - **Forward-Biased Safe Operating Area (FBSOA):** Bounded by maximum collector current $I_{CM}$, maximum power dissipation $P_D$, and maximum collector-emitter voltage $V_{CES}$. Modern trench IGBTs feature a square FBSOA up to short-circuit durations of $10\ \mu\text{s}$. - **Reverse-Biased Safe Operating Area (RBSOA):** Governs dynamic turn-off when clamping inductive voltage spikes. With proper gate resistance $R_g$, modern IGBTs offer full square RBSOA at twice the continuous rated collector current ($2 I_C$) up to $V_{CES}$.

Modern power electronic converters utilize diverse power semiconductor switches depending on the operating power level, voltage blocking requirement, switching frequency, and gate control complexity.

### 1. Device Physics and Operating Principles

#### A. #### B. Module 2

Controlled Rectifiers (Phase Controlled Converters)

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation & Analytical ID: PE-Q6

Explain the operation of a single-phase half-wave controlled rectifier with R and RL loads. Derive expressions for average and RMS output voltages, and explain the effect of a freewheeling diode. Explain the operation of a single-phase half-wave controlled rectifier with R and RL loads. Derive expressions for average and RMS output voltages, and explain the effect of a freewheeling diode. Explain the operation of a single-phase half-wave controlled rectifier with R and RL loads. Derive expressions for average and RMS output voltages, and explain the effect of a freewheeling diode.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha), \quad V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{(\pi - \alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha}, \quad i_o(\beta) = 0$$

1-Phase Half-Wave Controlled Rectifier. Inductive RL load extends conduction into negative half-cycle to extinction angle $\beta$. Freewheeling diode clamps $v_o \ge 0$, discharges inductive energy, boosts average DC voltage, and improves power factor.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha), \quad V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{(\pi - \alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha}, \quad i_o(\beta) = 0$$

1-Phase Half-Wave Controlled Rectifier. Inductive RL load extends conduction into negative half-cycle to extinction angle $\beta$. Freewheeling diode clamps $v_o \ge 0$, discharges inductive energy, boosts average DC voltage, and improves power factor.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha), \quad V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{(\pi - \alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha}, \quad i_o(\beta) = 0$$

1-Phase Half-Wave Controlled Rectifier. Inductive RL load extends conduction into negative half-cycle to extinction angle $\beta$. Freewheeling diode clamps $v_o \ge 0$, discharges inductive energy, boosts average DC voltage, and improves power factor.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The single-phase half-wave controlled converter represents the most fundamental phase-controlled topology, employing a single thyristor in series with an AC voltage source $v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t$ and a load.

### 1. Resistive (R) Load When the load is purely resistive, the thyristor is forward-biased during the positive half-cycle ($0 \le \omega t \le \pi$). When a gate trigger pulse is applied at firing angle $\omega t = \alpha$, the SCR turns on instantly. Conduction ceases naturally at $\omega t = \pi$ when the supply voltage drops to zero and reverses (natural or line commutation).

- **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^\pi = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\pi - (-\cos\alpha)] = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} (1 + \cos\alpha)$$

- **RMS Output Voltage ($V_{rms}$):** $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m^2 \sin^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\int_\alpha^\pi \frac{1 - \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{\left[ \omega t - \frac{\sin 2\omega t}{2} \right]_\alpha^\pi} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{(\pi - \alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha}$$

### 2. Inductive ($R-L$) Load without Freewheeling Diode With an inductive load, when the thyristor is triggered at $\omega t = \alpha$, load current $i_o(t)$ begins to rise. Energy is stored in the magnetic field of the inductor ($E_L = \frac{1}{2} L i^2$).

``` v_s = V_m sin(wt) ───[ SCR ]───┬────────o + │ [R] │ v_o(t) [L] │ ──────────────┴────────o - ```

The governing differential equation during conduction ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \beta$) is: $$L \frac{di_o}{dt} + R i_o = V_m \sin\omega t$$

Solving this linear non-homogeneous differential equation, the total current consists of steady-state and transient components: $$i_o(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\omega t - \phi) + A e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}\omega t}$$ where $Z = \sqrt{R^2 + (\omega L)^2}$ and $\phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega L}{R}\right)$.

Applying initial boundary condition $i_o(\alpha) = 0$: $$0 = \frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\alpha - \phi) + A e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}\alpha} \implies A = -\frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{\frac{R}{\omega L}\alpha}$$

Substituting constant $A$ back into the current equation: $$i_o(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{Z}\left[ \sin(\omega t - \phi) - \sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}(\omega t - \alpha)} \right]$$

- **Extinction Angle ($\beta$):** At $\omega t = \pi$, the supply voltage reverses, but the current cannot drop instantaneously to zero due to inductive opposition. The collapsing magnetic field self-induces an opposing electromotive force ($v_L = -L \frac{di}{dt}$), maintaining the anode positive relative to the cathode. Conduction continues into the negative half-cycle until all stored magnetic energy is completely dissipated at $\omega t = \beta$ ($\beta > \pi$), termed the **extinction angle**. At $\omega t = \beta$, $i_o(\beta) = 0$: $$\sin(\beta - \phi) = \sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}(\beta - \alpha)}$$

- **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\beta V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^\beta = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(\cos\alpha - \cos\beta)$$ Because $\beta > \pi$, the negative voltage excursion between $\pi$ and $\beta$ substantially degrades the net DC average voltage.

### 3. $R-L$ Load with Freewheeling Diode (FD) To eliminate the undesirable negative voltage loop, a freewheeling diode ($D_{fw}$) is connected in inverse parallel across the $R-L$ load.

``` v_s ───[ SCR ]───┬───────────────o + │ │ ┌─┴─┐ [R] │FD │ │ v_o(t) └─┬─┘ [L] │ │ ─────────────┴───────┴───────o - ```

- **Operation:** 1. From $\omega t = \alpha$ to $\pi$: The thyristor conducts, $v_o = v_s$, and the diode is reverse-biased ($v_{FD} = -v_s$). 2. At $\omega t = \pi$: Supply voltage crosses zero and turns negative. This forward-biases the freewheeling diode, which turns on automatically. 3. The load voltage is clamped to zero: $$v_o(t) = 0 \quad (\pi \le \omega t \le \beta)$$ 4. The thyristor cathode potential becomes higher than its anode, forcing the SCR to turn off by line commutation at $\omega t = \pi$. 5. The load current now recirculates (freewheels) entirely through the diode and load resistance, governed by: $$L \frac{di}{dt} + R i = 0 \implies i_o(t) = I_0 e^{-\frac{R}{L}t}$$

- **Average Output Voltage:** Since output voltage is positive from $\alpha$ to $\pi$ and identically zero from $\pi$ to $2\pi$: $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$$

- **Key Advantages of Freewheeling Diode:** 1. Prevents negative voltage excursion, substantially increasing average output voltage $V_{dc}$. 2. Provides an alternate discharge path for inductive energy, smoothing load current and reducing ripple factor. 3. Relieves the thyristor of reverse-voltage stress during carrier sweep-out. 4. Improves the input displacement factor and overall power factor of the converter.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The single-phase half-wave controlled converter represents the most fundamental phase-controlled topology, employing a single thyristor in series with an AC voltage source $v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t$ and a load.

### 1. Resistive (R) Load When the load is purely resistive, the thyristor is forward-biased during the positive half-cycle ($0 \le \omega t \le \pi$). When a gate trigger pulse is applied at firing angle $\omega t = \alpha$, the SCR turns on instantly. Conduction ceases naturally at $\omega t = \pi$ when the supply voltage drops to zero and reverses (natural or line commutation).

- **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^\pi = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\pi - (-\cos\alpha)] = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} (1 + \cos\alpha)$$

- **RMS Output Voltage ($V_{rms}$):** $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m^2 \sin^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\int_\alpha^\pi \frac{1 - \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{\left[ \omega t - \frac{\sin 2\omega t}{2} \right]_\alpha^\pi} = \frac{V_m}{2\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{(\pi - \alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha}$$

### 2. Inductive ($R-L$) Load without Freewheeling Diode With an inductive load, when the thyristor is triggered at $\omega t = \alpha$, load current $i_o(t)$ begins to rise. Energy is stored in the magnetic field of the inductor ($E_L = \frac{1}{2} L i^2$).

``` v_s = V_m sin(wt) ───[ SCR ]───┬────────o + │ [R] │ v_o(t) [L] │ ──────────────┴────────o - ```

The governing differential equation during conduction ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \beta$) is: $$L \frac{di_o}{dt} + R i_o = V_m \sin\omega t$$

Solving this linear non-homogeneous differential equation, the total current consists of steady-state and transient components: $$i_o(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\omega t - \phi) + A e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}\omega t}$$ where $Z = \sqrt{R^2 + (\omega L)^2}$ and $\phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega L}{R}\right)$.

Applying initial boundary condition $i_o(\alpha) = 0$: $$0 = \frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\alpha - \phi) + A e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}\alpha} \implies A = -\frac{V_m}{Z}\sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{\frac{R}{\omega L}\alpha}$$

Substituting constant $A$ back into the current equation: $$i_o(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{Z}\left[ \sin(\omega t - \phi) - \sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}(\omega t - \alpha)} \right]$$

- **Extinction Angle ($\beta$):** At $\omega t = \pi$, the supply voltage reverses, but the current cannot drop instantaneously to zero due to inductive opposition. The collapsing magnetic field self-induces an opposing electromotive force ($v_L = -L \frac{di}{dt}$), maintaining the anode positive relative to the cathode. Conduction continues into the negative half-cycle until all stored magnetic energy is completely dissipated at $\omega t = \beta$ ($\beta > \pi$), termed the **extinction angle**. At $\omega t = \beta$, $i_o(\beta) = 0$: $$\sin(\beta - \phi) = \sin(\alpha - \phi) e^{-\frac{R}{\omega L}(\beta - \alpha)}$$

- **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\beta V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^\beta = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(\cos\alpha - \cos\beta)$$ Because $\beta > \pi$, the negative voltage excursion between $\pi$ and $\beta$ substantially degrades the net DC average voltage.

### 3. $R-L$ Load with Freewheeling Diode (FD) To eliminate the undesirable negative voltage loop, a freewheeling diode ($D_{fw}$) is connected in inverse parallel across the $R-L$ load.

``` v_s ───[ SCR ]───┬───────────────o + │ │ ┌─┴─┐ [R] │FD │ │ v_o(t) └─┬─┘ [L] │ │ ─────────────┴───────┴───────o - ```

- **Operation:** 1. From $\omega t = \alpha$ to $\pi$: The thyristor conducts, $v_o = v_s$, and the diode is reverse-biased ($v_{FD} = -v_s$). 2. At $\omega t = \pi$: Supply voltage crosses zero and turns negative. This forward-biases the freewheeling diode, which turns on automatically. 3. The load voltage is clamped to zero: $$v_o(t) = 0 \quad (\pi \le \omega t \le \beta)$$ 4. The thyristor cathode potential becomes higher than its anode, forcing the SCR to turn off by line commutation at $\omega t = \pi$. 5. The load current now recirculates (freewheels) entirely through the diode and load resistance, governed by: $$L \frac{di}{dt} + R i = 0 \implies i_o(t) = I_0 e^{-\frac{R}{L}t}$$

- **Average Output Voltage:** Since output voltage is positive from $\alpha$ to $\pi$ and identically zero from $\pi$ to $2\pi$: $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_\alpha^\pi V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{2\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$$

- **Key Advantages of Freewheeling Diode:** 1. Prevents negative voltage excursion, substantially increasing average output voltage $V_{dc}$. 2. Provides an alternate discharge path for inductive energy, smoothing load current and reducing ripple factor. 3. Relieves the thyristor of reverse-voltage stress during carrier sweep-out. 4. Improves the input displacement factor and overall power factor of the converter.

Derive the expression for average and RMS output voltages of a single-phase fully controlled bridge rectifier feeding an RLE load. Distinguish between continuous and discontinuous conduction modes. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha, \quad V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} = V_s, \quad I_{dc} = \frac{V_{dc} - E}{R}$$

1-Phase Full Converter with RLE load operates in Quadrant I ($0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$, Rectification) and Quadrant IV ($90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$, Inversion). Line commutation transfers current between SCR pairs. Discontinuous conduction occurs at light loads.
