Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The single-phase fully controlled bridge rectifier (full converter) consists of four thyristors ($T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$) in a Graetz bridge configuration feeding an active $R-L-E$ load, representing the armature circuit of a DC motor with back EMF $E$.

``` ┌──────────┬──────────┐ │ T1 │ T3 │ ┌────o─►|─┐ ┌─o─►|─┐ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ v_s(t) └────┼───┘ └────┼─────┘ │ │ │ └─────────┼──────────┤ ┌────┴────┐┌────┴────┐ │ T4 ││ T2 │ └─►|─o────┘└─►|─o────┘ │ │ └────┬─────┘ │ ┌──┴──┐ │ [R] │ │ [L] │ v_o(t), i_o(t) │ (E) │ └──┬──┘ │ ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation Under continuous conduction, load inductance $L$ is sufficiently large that load current $i_o(t)$ never decays to zero. - **Positive Half-Cycle ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$):** Thyristors $T_1$ and $T_2$ are triggered at $\omega t = \alpha$. They conduct simultaneously, applying AC supply voltage directly to the load: $$v_o(t) = v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t$$ - **Negative Half-Cycle ($\pi + \alpha \le \omega t \le 2\pi + \alpha$):** At $\omega t = \pi + \alpha$, gate firing pulses are applied to $T_3$ and $T_4$. Because supply voltage is negative ($v_s < 0$), firing $T_3$ and $T_4$ applies a reverse anode-to-cathode voltage across $T_1$ and $T_2$, commutating them off by line commutation. Now $T_3$ and $T_4$ connect the negative supply line to the load: $$v_o(t) = -v_s(t) = -V_m \sin\omega t$$

### 2. Mathematical Derivation of Average and RMS Output Voltages Since the output voltage waveform repeats every $\pi$ radians ($180^\circ$ symmetry): - **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(\pi+\alpha) - (-\cos\alpha) \right] = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha + \cos\alpha] = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

- **RMS Output Voltage ($V_{rms}$):** $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m^2 \sin^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{\int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} \frac{1 - \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\left[ \omega t - \frac{\sin 2\omega t}{2} \right]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\pi} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} = V_s$$ The RMS output voltage is independent of firing angle $\alpha$ and exactly equals the RMS AC supply voltage $V_s$.

- **Average Load Current ($I_{dc}$):** Applying KVL to the DC loop: $$V_{dc} = R I_{dc} + E \implies I_{dc} = \frac{V_{dc} - E}{R} = \frac{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - E}{R}$$

### 3. Two-Quadrant Operation (Rectification vs Inversion) The full converter operates in two quadrants on the $V_{dc}-I_{dc}$ plane: 1. **Quadrant I (Rectifier Mode, $0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha > 0 \implies V_{dc} > 0$. Since thyristors only conduct current unidirectionally, $I_{dc} > 0$. Net power $P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} > 0$. Power flows from AC supply to DC motor (motoring drive). 2. **Quadrant IV (Inverter Mode, $90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha < 0 \implies V_{dc} < 0$. Current cannot reverse ($I_{dc} > 0$). If the polarity of the DC source EMF $E$ is reversed (by reversing motor armature terminals during regenerative braking) such that $|E| > |V_{dc}|$, net power becomes negative: $$P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} < 0$$ Power flows from the DC machine back into the AC utility grid. This is termed a **line-commutated inverter**.

``` V_dc (Average Voltage) ▲ │ Quadrant I: Rectification (Motoring) │ P_dc > 0, 0 <= alpha < 90 deg │ ───────────────┼───────────────► I_dc (Current unidirectional) │ │ Quadrant IV: Inversion (Regeneration) │ P_dc < 0, 90 deg < alpha < 180 deg ▼ ```

### 4. Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM) When load current is low, or at high values of back EMF $E$, the load current drops to zero at extinction angle $\beta < \pi + \alpha$. During the idle interval $\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$, all four thyristors are off ($i_o = 0$), and the output voltage floats at the battery back EMF: $$v_o(t) = E \quad (\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha)$$

- **Average Voltage in DCM:** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \left[ \int_\alpha^\beta V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) + \int_\beta^{\pi+\alpha} E\,d(\omega t) \right]$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(\cos\alpha - \cos\beta) + \frac{E}{\pi}(\pi + \alpha - \beta)$$ - **Minimum Firing Angle ($\alpha_{\min}$):** To initiate conduction, the peak instantaneous AC supply voltage must exceed back EMF $E$: $$\alpha_{\min} = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{E}{V_m}\right)$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The single-phase fully controlled bridge rectifier (full converter) consists of four thyristors ($T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$) in a Graetz bridge configuration feeding an active $R-L-E$ load, representing the armature circuit of a DC motor with back EMF $E$.

``` ┌──────────┬──────────┐ │ T1 │ T3 │ ┌────o─►|─┐ ┌─o─►|─┐ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ v_s(t) └────┼───┘ └────┼─────┘ │ │ │ └─────────┼──────────┤ ┌────┴────┐┌────┴────┐ │ T4 ││ T2 │ └─►|─o────┘└─►|─o────┘ │ │ └────┬─────┘ │ ┌──┴──┐ │ [R] │ │ [L] │ v_o(t), i_o(t) │ (E) │ └──┬──┘ │ ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation Under continuous conduction, load inductance $L$ is sufficiently large that load current $i_o(t)$ never decays to zero. - **Positive Half-Cycle ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$):** Thyristors $T_1$ and $T_2$ are triggered at $\omega t = \alpha$. They conduct simultaneously, applying AC supply voltage directly to the load: $$v_o(t) = v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t$$ - **Negative Half-Cycle ($\pi + \alpha \le \omega t \le 2\pi + \alpha$):** At $\omega t = \pi + \alpha$, gate firing pulses are applied to $T_3$ and $T_4$. Because supply voltage is negative ($v_s < 0$), firing $T_3$ and $T_4$ applies a reverse anode-to-cathode voltage across $T_1$ and $T_2$, commutating them off by line commutation. Now $T_3$ and $T_4$ connect the negative supply line to the load: $$v_o(t) = -v_s(t) = -V_m \sin\omega t$$

### 2. Mathematical Derivation of Average and RMS Output Voltages Since the output voltage waveform repeats every $\pi$ radians ($180^\circ$ symmetry): - **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(\pi+\alpha) - (-\cos\alpha) \right] = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha + \cos\alpha] = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

- **RMS Output Voltage ($V_{rms}$):** $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m^2 \sin^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{\int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} \frac{1 - \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\left[ \omega t - \frac{\sin 2\omega t}{2} \right]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\pi} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} = V_s$$ The RMS output voltage is independent of firing angle $\alpha$ and exactly equals the RMS AC supply voltage $V_s$.

- **Average Load Current ($I_{dc}$):** Applying KVL to the DC loop: $$V_{dc} = R I_{dc} + E \implies I_{dc} = \frac{V_{dc} - E}{R} = \frac{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - E}{R}$$

### 3. Two-Quadrant Operation (Rectification vs Inversion) The full converter operates in two quadrants on the $V_{dc}-I_{dc}$ plane: 1. **Quadrant I (Rectifier Mode, $0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha > 0 \implies V_{dc} > 0$. Since thyristors only conduct current unidirectionally, $I_{dc} > 0$. Net power $P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} > 0$. Power flows from AC supply to DC motor (motoring drive). 2. **Quadrant IV (Inverter Mode, $90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha < 0 \implies V_{dc} < 0$. Current cannot reverse ($I_{dc} > 0$). If the polarity of the DC source EMF $E$ is reversed (by reversing motor armature terminals during regenerative braking) such that $|E| > |V_{dc}|$, net power becomes negative: $$P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} < 0$$ Power flows from the DC machine back into the AC utility grid. This is termed a **line-commutated inverter**.

``` V_dc (Average Voltage) ▲ │ Quadrant I: Rectification (Motoring) │ P_dc > 0, 0 <= alpha < 90 deg │ ───────────────┼───────────────► I_dc (Current unidirectional) │ │ Quadrant IV: Inversion (Regeneration) │ P_dc < 0, 90 deg < alpha < 180 deg ▼ ```

### 4. Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM) When load current is low, or at high values of back EMF $E$, the load current drops to zero at extinction angle $\beta < \pi + \alpha$. During the idle interval $\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$, all four thyristors are off ($i_o = 0$), and the output voltage floats at the battery back EMF: $$v_o(t) = E \quad (\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha)$$

- **Average Voltage in DCM:** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \left[ \int_\alpha^\beta V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) + \int_\beta^{\pi+\alpha} E\,d(\omega t) \right]$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(\cos\alpha - \cos\beta) + \frac{E}{\pi}(\pi + \alpha - \beta)$$ - **Minimum Firing Angle ($\alpha_{\min}$):** To initiate conduction, the peak instantaneous AC supply voltage must exceed back EMF $E$: $$\alpha_{\min} = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{E}{V_m}\right)$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The single-phase fully controlled bridge rectifier (full converter) consists of four thyristors ($T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$) in a Graetz bridge configuration feeding an active $R-L-E$ load, representing the armature circuit of a DC motor with back EMF $E$.

``` ┌──────────┬──────────┐ │ T1 │ T3 │ ┌────o─►|─┐ ┌─o─►|─┐ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ v_s(t) └────┼───┘ └────┼─────┘ │ │ │ └─────────┼──────────┤ ┌────┴────┐┌────┴────┐ │ T4 ││ T2 │ └─►|─o────┘└─►|─o────┘ │ │ └────┬─────┘ │ ┌──┴──┐ │ [R] │ │ [L] │ v_o(t), i_o(t) │ (E) │ └──┬──┘ │ ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation Under continuous conduction, load inductance $L$ is sufficiently large that load current $i_o(t)$ never decays to zero. - **Positive Half-Cycle ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$):** Thyristors $T_1$ and $T_2$ are triggered at $\omega t = \alpha$. They conduct simultaneously, applying AC supply voltage directly to the load: $$v_o(t) = v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t$$ - **Negative Half-Cycle ($\pi + \alpha \le \omega t \le 2\pi + \alpha$):** At $\omega t = \pi + \alpha$, gate firing pulses are applied to $T_3$ and $T_4$. Because supply voltage is negative ($v_s < 0$), firing $T_3$ and $T_4$ applies a reverse anode-to-cathode voltage across $T_1$ and $T_2$, commutating them off by line commutation. Now $T_3$ and $T_4$ connect the negative supply line to the load: $$v_o(t) = -v_s(t) = -V_m \sin\omega t$$

### 2. Mathematical Derivation of Average and RMS Output Voltages Since the output voltage waveform repeats every $\pi$ radians ($180^\circ$ symmetry): - **Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$):** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} \left[ -\cos(\pi+\alpha) - (-\cos\alpha) \right] = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha + \cos\alpha] = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

- **RMS Output Voltage ($V_{rms}$):** $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} V_m^2 \sin^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{\pi}} \sqrt{\int_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha} \frac{1 - \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\left[ \omega t - \frac{\sin 2\omega t}{2} \right]_\alpha^{\pi+\alpha}} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \sqrt{\pi} = \frac{V_m}{\sqrt{2}} = V_s$$ The RMS output voltage is independent of firing angle $\alpha$ and exactly equals the RMS AC supply voltage $V_s$.

- **Average Load Current ($I_{dc}$):** Applying KVL to the DC loop: $$V_{dc} = R I_{dc} + E \implies I_{dc} = \frac{V_{dc} - E}{R} = \frac{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - E}{R}$$

### 3. Two-Quadrant Operation (Rectification vs Inversion) The full converter operates in two quadrants on the $V_{dc}-I_{dc}$ plane: 1. **Quadrant I (Rectifier Mode, $0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha > 0 \implies V_{dc} > 0$. Since thyristors only conduct current unidirectionally, $I_{dc} > 0$. Net power $P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} > 0$. Power flows from AC supply to DC motor (motoring drive). 2. **Quadrant IV (Inverter Mode, $90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$):** $\cos\alpha < 0 \implies V_{dc} < 0$. Current cannot reverse ($I_{dc} > 0$). If the polarity of the DC source EMF $E$ is reversed (by reversing motor armature terminals during regenerative braking) such that $|E| > |V_{dc}|$, net power becomes negative: $$P_{dc} = V_{dc} I_{dc} < 0$$ Power flows from the DC machine back into the AC utility grid. This is termed a **line-commutated inverter**.

``` V_dc (Average Voltage) ▲ │ Quadrant I: Rectification (Motoring) │ P_dc > 0, 0 <= alpha < 90 deg │ ───────────────┼───────────────► I_dc (Current unidirectional) │ │ Quadrant IV: Inversion (Regeneration) │ P_dc < 0, 90 deg < alpha < 180 deg ▼ ```

### 4. Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM) When load current is low, or at high values of back EMF $E$, the load current drops to zero at extinction angle $\beta < \pi + \alpha$. During the idle interval $\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha$, all four thyristors are off ($i_o = 0$), and the output voltage floats at the battery back EMF: $$v_o(t) = E \quad (\beta \le \omega t \le \pi + \alpha)$$

- **Average Voltage in DCM:** $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi} \left[ \int_\alpha^\beta V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) + \int_\beta^{\pi+\alpha} E\,d(\omega t) \right]$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(\cos\alpha - \cos\beta) + \frac{E}{\pi}(\pi + \alpha - \beta)$$ - **Minimum Firing Angle ($\alpha_{\min}$):** To initiate conduction, the peak instantaneous AC supply voltage must exceed back EMF $E$: $$\alpha_{\min} = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{E}{V_m}\right)$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\text{DF} = \cos\alpha, \quad \text{THD} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}}\right)^2 - 1} \approx 48.34\%, \quad \text{PF} = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To evaluate the power quality, grid distortion, and rectification efficiency of phase-controlled converters, standardized performance parameters are established.

### 1. Fundamental Definitions and Mathematical Formulations

#### A. Input Displacement Factor (DF) The Displacement Factor is the cosine of the phase displacement angle $\phi_1$ between the fundamental component of AC input current $i_{s1}(t)$ and the sinusoidal AC input voltage $v_s(t)$: $$\text{DF} = \cos\phi_1$$ For a single-phase fully controlled converter with ripple-free DC load current $I_{dc}$, the fundamental current lags the supply voltage by the firing angle $\alpha$: $$\phi_1 = \alpha \implies \text{DF} = \cos\alpha$$

#### B. Harmonic Factor (HF) / Total Harmonic Distortion (THD) The Harmonic Factor measures the harmonic content present in the input supply current relative to the fundamental component: $$\text{THD} = \text{HF} = \frac{\sqrt{I_s^2 - I_{s1}^2}}{I_{s1}} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{I_s}{I_{s1}}\right)^2 - 1}$$ where $I_s$ is the total RMS supply current and $I_{s1}$ is the RMS value of the fundamental current.

For a 1-phase full converter with ripple-free DC load current $I_{dc}$: - Supply current is a square wave of amplitude $\pm I_{dc}$: $$I_s = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}\int_0^\pi I_{dc}^2\,d(\omega t)} = I_{dc}$$ - Fourier series expansion of supply current $i_s(t)$: $$i_s(t) = \sum_{n=1,3,5\dots}^\infty \frac{4 I_{dc}}{n \pi} \sin\left[n(\omega t - \alpha)\right]$$ - Fundamental RMS current ($n = 1$): $$I_{s1} = \frac{4 I_{dc}}{\pi \sqrt{2}} = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi} I_{dc} \approx 0.9003 I_{dc}$$

Substituting $I_s$ and $I_{s1}$ into the THD formulation: $$\text{THD} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{I_{dc}}{\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}I_{dc}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\frac{\pi^2}{8} - 1} \approx \sqrt{1.2337 - 1} = \sqrt{0.2337} \approx 0.4834 \ (48.34\%)$$

#### C. Input Power Factor (PF) The overall power factor is the ratio of real active input power to total apparent volt-amperes: $$\text{PF} = \frac{P_{in}}{S_{in}} = \frac{V_s I_{s1} \cos\phi_1}{V_s I_s} = \left(\frac{I_{s1}}{I_s}\right) \cos\phi_1 = g \cdot \text{DF}$$ where $g = I_{s1} / I_s$ is defined as the **Current Distortion Factor**. For the 1-phase full converter: $$g = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi} \approx 0.9003 \implies \text{PF} = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha \approx 0.9 \cos\alpha$$

#### D. Form Factor (FF) and Voltage Ripple Factor (RF) - **Form Factor (FF):** Ratio of RMS output voltage to average DC output voltage: $$\text{FF} = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{dc}} = \frac{V_m/\sqrt{2}}{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha} = \frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}\cos\alpha}$$ - **Ripple Factor (RF):** Measure of residual AC ripple voltage on the DC output: $$\text{RF} = \frac{V_{ac}}{V_{dc}} = \frac{\sqrt{V_{rms}^2 - V_{dc}^2}}{V_{dc}} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{V_{rms}}{V_{dc}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\text{FF}^2 - 1}$$

### 2. Comprehensive Comparison: Semi-Converter vs Full-Converter

| Metric / Parameter | 1-Phase Semi-Converter | 1-Phase Full-Converter | | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Topology** | 2 SCRs + 2 Diodes (or FD) | 4 SCRs (Fully controlled) | | **Quadrants** | 1 Quadrant ($V_{dc} \ge 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | 2 Quadrants ($V_{dc} \gtrless 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | | **Average Output Voltage** | $V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$ | $V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **RMS Output Voltage** | $V_s \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}[(\pi-\alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha]}$ | $V_{rms} = V_s$ (Constant) | | **Inversion Capability** | No (Cannot regenerate) | Yes (Regeneration for $\alpha > 90^\circ$) | | **Input Current Waveform** | Quasi-square with zero gaps | Pure square wave ($I_{dc}$ to $-I_{dc}$) | | **Displacement Factor (DF)**| $\cos(\alpha/2)$ | $\cos\alpha$ | | **Input Power Factor (PF)** | $\frac{\sqrt{2}(1 + \cos\alpha)}{\sqrt{\pi(\pi - \alpha)}}$ | $\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **Reactive Power Demand** | Much lower (Freewheeling diode) | High, especially near $\alpha \approx 90^\circ$ | | **Circuit Cost & Control** | Cheaper (2 gate drivers only) | Higher (4 isolated gate drivers) |

**Key Takeaway:** At any firing angle $\alpha > 0$, the semi-converter exhibits a significantly superior power factor and lower reactive power consumption because freewheeling action bypasses the AC source during $(0 \le \omega t \le \alpha)$, eliminating reactive circulating current.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

To evaluate the power quality, grid distortion, and rectification efficiency of phase-controlled converters, standardized performance parameters are established.

### 1. Fundamental Definitions and Mathematical Formulations

#### A. Input Displacement Factor (DF) The Displacement Factor is the cosine of the phase displacement angle $\phi_1$ between the fundamental component of AC input current $i_{s1}(t)$ and the sinusoidal AC input voltage $v_s(t)$: $$\text{DF} = \cos\phi_1$$ For a single-phase fully controlled converter with ripple-free DC load current $I_{dc}$, the fundamental current lags the supply voltage by the firing angle $\alpha$: $$\phi_1 = \alpha \implies \text{DF} = \cos\alpha$$

#### B. Harmonic Factor (HF) / Total Harmonic Distortion (THD) The Harmonic Factor measures the harmonic content present in the input supply current relative to the fundamental component: $$\text{THD} = \text{HF} = \frac{\sqrt{I_s^2 - I_{s1}^2}}{I_{s1}} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{I_s}{I_{s1}}\right)^2 - 1}$$ where $I_s$ is the total RMS supply current and $I_{s1}$ is the RMS value of the fundamental current.

For a 1-phase full converter with ripple-free DC load current $I_{dc}$: - Supply current is a square wave of amplitude $\pm I_{dc}$: $$I_s = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}\int_0^\pi I_{dc}^2\,d(\omega t)} = I_{dc}$$ - Fourier series expansion of supply current $i_s(t)$: $$i_s(t) = \sum_{n=1,3,5\dots}^\infty \frac{4 I_{dc}}{n \pi} \sin\left[n(\omega t - \alpha)\right]$$ - Fundamental RMS current ($n = 1$): $$I_{s1} = \frac{4 I_{dc}}{\pi \sqrt{2}} = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi} I_{dc} \approx 0.9003 I_{dc}$$

Substituting $I_s$ and $I_{s1}$ into the THD formulation: $$\text{THD} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{I_{dc}}{\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}I_{dc}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\frac{\pi^2}{8} - 1} \approx \sqrt{1.2337 - 1} = \sqrt{0.2337} \approx 0.4834 \ (48.34\%)$$

#### C. Input Power Factor (PF) The overall power factor is the ratio of real active input power to total apparent volt-amperes: $$\text{PF} = \frac{P_{in}}{S_{in}} = \frac{V_s I_{s1} \cos\phi_1}{V_s I_s} = \left(\frac{I_{s1}}{I_s}\right) \cos\phi_1 = g \cdot \text{DF}$$ where $g = I_{s1} / I_s$ is defined as the **Current Distortion Factor**. For the 1-phase full converter: $$g = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi} \approx 0.9003 \implies \text{PF} = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha \approx 0.9 \cos\alpha$$

#### D. Form Factor (FF) and Voltage Ripple Factor (RF) - **Form Factor (FF):** Ratio of RMS output voltage to average DC output voltage: $$\text{FF} = \frac{V_{rms}}{V_{dc}} = \frac{V_m/\sqrt{2}}{\frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha} = \frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}\cos\alpha}$$ - **Ripple Factor (RF):** Measure of residual AC ripple voltage on the DC output: $$\text{RF} = \frac{V_{ac}}{V_{dc}} = \frac{\sqrt{V_{rms}^2 - V_{dc}^2}}{V_{dc}} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{V_{rms}}{V_{dc}}\right)^2 - 1} = \sqrt{\text{FF}^2 - 1}$$

### 2. Comprehensive Comparison: Semi-Converter vs Full-Converter

| Metric / Parameter | 1-Phase Semi-Converter | 1-Phase Full-Converter | | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Topology** | 2 SCRs + 2 Diodes (or FD) | 4 SCRs (Fully controlled) | | **Quadrants** | 1 Quadrant ($V_{dc} \ge 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | 2 Quadrants ($V_{dc} \gtrless 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | | **Average Output Voltage** | $V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$ | $V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **RMS Output Voltage** | $V_s \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}[(\pi-\alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha]}$ | $V_{rms} = V_s$ (Constant) | | **Inversion Capability** | No (Cannot regenerate) | Yes (Regeneration for $\alpha > 90^\circ$) | | **Input Current Waveform** | Quasi-square with zero gaps | Pure square wave ($I_{dc}$ to $-I_{dc}$) | | **Displacement Factor (DF)**| $\cos(\alpha/2)$ | $\cos\alpha$ | | **Input Power Factor (PF)** | $\frac{\sqrt{2}(1 + \cos\alpha)}{\sqrt{\pi(\pi - \alpha)}}$ | $\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **Reactive Power Demand** | Much lower (Freewheeling diode) | High, especially near $\alpha \approx 90^\circ$ | | **Circuit Cost & Control** | Cheaper (2 gate drivers only) | Higher (4 isolated gate drivers) |

**Key Takeaway:** At any firing angle $\alpha > 0$, the semi-converter exhibits a significantly superior power factor and lower reactive power consumption because freewheeling action bypasses the AC source during $(0 \le \omega t \le \alpha)$, eliminating reactive circulating current.

### 2. Comprehensive Comparison: Semi-Converter vs Full-Converter

| Metric / Parameter | 1-Phase Semi-Converter | 1-Phase Full-Converter | | :--- | :--- | :--- | | **Topology** | 2 SCRs + 2 Diodes (or FD) | 4 SCRs (Fully controlled) | | **Quadrants** | 1 Quadrant ($V_{dc} \ge 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | 2 Quadrants ($V_{dc} \gtrless 0, I_{dc} \ge 0$) | | **Average Output Voltage** | $V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi}(1 + \cos\alpha)$ | $V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **RMS Output Voltage** | $V_s \sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}[(\pi-\alpha) + \frac{1}{2}\sin 2\alpha]}$ | $V_{rms} = V_s$ (Constant) | | **Inversion Capability** | No (Cannot regenerate) | Yes (Regeneration for $\alpha > 90^\circ$) | | **Input Current Waveform** | Quasi-square with zero gaps | Pure square wave ($I_{dc}$ to $-I_{dc}$) | | **Displacement Factor (DF)**| $\cos(\alpha/2)$ | $\cos\alpha$ | | **Input Power Factor (PF)** | $\frac{\sqrt{2}(1 + \cos\alpha)}{\sqrt{\pi(\pi - \alpha)}}$ | $\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$ | | **Reactive Power Demand** | Much lower (Freewheeling diode) | High, especially near $\alpha \approx 90^\circ$ | | **Circuit Cost & Control** | Cheaper (2 gate drivers only) | Higher (4 isolated gate drivers) |

**Key Takeaway:** At any firing angle $\alpha > 0$, the semi-converter exhibits a significantly superior power factor and lower reactive power consumption because freewheeling action bypasses the AC source during $(0 \le \omega t \le \alpha)$, eliminating reactive circulating current.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 2.3: Input Current Harmonic Decomposition, Displacement Angle, and Performance Comparison of Semi vs Full Converters — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The three-phase fully controlled bridge converter (6-pulse converter) is the standard workhorse topology for high-power DC drives, electrochemical refining plants, and HVDC converter terminals.

### 1. Circuit Configuration and Conduction Sequence The circuit consists of 6 thyristors connected across a balanced 3-phase AC supply ($v_a, v_b, v_c$): - **Positive Group (Upper SCRs):** $T_1$ (phase $a$), $T_3$ (phase $b$), $T_5$ (phase $c$). The thyristor with the most positive instantaneous anode voltage conducts. - **Negative Group (Lower SCRs):** $T_4$ (phase $a$), $T_6$ (phase $b$), $T_2$ (phase $c$). The thyristor with the most negative cathode voltage conducts.

``` ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┐ │ T1 │ T3 │ T5 │ ┌────o─►|─┐ ┌─o─►|─┐ ┌─o─►|─┐ │ Va ────┤ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ └────┼──┘ └────┼──┘ └────┼────┘ Vb ────┼─────────┼─────────┤ │ │ │ │ │ Vc ────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤ │ ┌────┴───┐┌────┴───┐┌────┴───┐ └────┤ T4 ││ T6 ││ T2 │ └─►|─o───┘└─►|─o───┘└─►|─o───┘ │ │ │ └─────────┴────┬────┘ │ [ Load ] │ ```

- **Triggering Sequence:** Thyristors are fired in numerical index order at uniform intervals of $60^\circ$ ($\pi/3$ rad): $$T_1 \to T_2 \to T_3 \to T_4 \to T_5 \to T_6 \to T_1$$ - **Conduction Angle:** Each thyristor conducts for $120^\circ$ ($2\pi/3$ rad). - At any operating instant, exactly two thyristors conduct simultaneously: one from the upper group and one from the lower group. The load is connected directly across the corresponding line-to-line voltage. - **Conduction Pairs:** - $60^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 120^\circ + \alpha$: $T_1$ and $T_6$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ab}$) - $120^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 180^\circ + \alpha$: $T_1$ and $T_2$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ac}$) - $180^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 240^\circ + \alpha$: $T_3$ and $T_2$ conduct ($v_o = v_{bc}$) - $240^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 300^\circ + \alpha$: $T_3$ and $T_4$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ba}$) - $300^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 360^\circ + \alpha$: $T_5$ and $T_4$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ca}$) - $0^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 60^\circ + \alpha$: $T_5$ and $T_6$ conduct ($v_o = v_{cb}$)

### 2. Mathematical Derivation of Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$) Because the output voltage waveform repeats every $60^\circ$ ($\pi/3$ rad), the average voltage is evaluated over one $60^\circ$ conduction period. Let the line-to-line voltage $v_{ab}(t)$ be defined with reference to the peak line voltage $V_{ml} = \sqrt{2} V_L$: $$v_{ab}(\omega t) = V_{ml} \cos\omega t$$ The natural commutation point occurs at $\omega t = -30^\circ$ ($-\pi/6$). When fired with a delay angle $\alpha$, the conduction interval for line pair $v_{ab}$ spans from $-\pi/6 + \alpha$ to $+\pi/6 + \alpha$.

Evaluating the average DC voltage: $$V_{dc} = \frac{1}{\pi/3} \int_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha} V_{ml} \cos\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} [\sin\omega t]_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha}$$ $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} \left[ \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{6} + \alpha\right) - \sin\left(-\frac{\pi}{6} + \alpha\right) \right]$$

Applying the trigonometric identity $\sin(A + B) - \sin(B - A) = 2 \sin A \cos B$: $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} \left[ 2 \sin\frac{\pi}{6} \cos\alpha \right] = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} \left[ 2 \times \frac{1}{2} \cos\alpha \right] = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

Expressing in terms of RMS line voltage ($V_{ml} = \sqrt{2} V_L$): $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 \sqrt{2} V_L}{\pi}\cos\alpha \approx 1.35 V_L \cos\alpha$$

Expressing in terms of RMS phase voltage ($V_L = \sqrt{3} V_{ph}$): $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 \sqrt{6} V_{ph}}{\pi}\cos\alpha \approx 2.34 V_{ph} \cos\alpha$$

### 3. RMS Output Voltage Derivation $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi} \int_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha} V_{ml}^2 \cos^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi} \int_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha} \frac{1 + \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{1}{2} + \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}\cos 2\alpha}$$ At $\alpha = 0^\circ$: $$V_{rms} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{1}{2} + \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}} = \sqrt{2} V_L \times 0.9558 = 1.655 V_{ph}$$

### 4. Rectifier Mode vs Inverter Mode - For $0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$: $\cos\alpha > 0 \implies V_{dc} > 0$. Real power flows from AC line to DC load (motoring). - At $\alpha = 90^\circ$: $V_{dc} = 0$. - For $90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$: $\cos\alpha < 0 \implies V_{dc} < 0$. With reversed DC source EMF ($E > |V_{dc}|$), the converter operates as a line-commutated inverter, feeding active power from DC side into the 3-phase grid.

The load is connected directly across the corresponding line-to-line voltage. - **Conduction Pairs:** - $60^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 120^\circ + \alpha$: $T_1$ and $T_6$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ab}$) - $120^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 180^\circ + \alpha$: $T_1$ and $T_2$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ac}$) - $180^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 240^\circ + \alpha$: $T_3$ and $T_2$ conduct ($v_o = v_{bc}$) - $240^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 300^\circ + \alpha$: $T_3$ and $T_4$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ba}$) - $300^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 360^\circ + \alpha$: $T_5$ and $T_4$ conduct ($v_o = v_{ca}$) - $0^\circ + \alpha \text{ to } 60^\circ + \alpha$: $T_5$ and $T_6$ conduct ($v_o = v_{cb}$)

Mathematical Derivation of Average Output Voltage ($V_{dc}$) Because the output voltage waveform repeats every $60^\circ$ ($\pi/3$ rad), the average voltage is evaluated over one $60^\circ$ conduction period. Let the line-to-line voltage $v_{ab}(t)$ be defined with reference to the peak line voltage $V_{ml} = \sqrt{2} V_L$: $$v_{ab}(\omega t) = V_{ml} \cos\omega t$$ The natural commutation point occurs at $\omega t = -30^\circ$ ($-\pi/6$). Applying the trigonometric identity $\sin(A + B) - \sin(B - A) = 2 \sin A \cos B$: $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} \left[ 2 \sin\frac{\pi}{6} \cos\alpha \right] = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi} \left[ 2 \times \frac{1}{2} \cos\alpha \right] = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi}\cos\alpha$$

Expressing in terms of RMS phase voltage ($V_L = \sqrt{3} V_{ph}$): $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 \sqrt{6} V_{ph}}{\pi}\cos\alpha \approx 2.34 V_{ph} \cos\alpha$$

### 3. RMS Output Voltage Derivation $$V_{rms} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi} \int_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha} V_{ml}^2 \cos^2\omega t\,d(\omega t)} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi} \int_{-\pi/6 + \alpha}^{\pi/6 + \alpha} \frac{1 + \cos 2\omega t}{2}\,d(\omega t)}$$ $$V_{rms} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{1}{2} + \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}\cos 2\alpha}$$ At $\alpha = 0^\circ$: $$V_{rms} = V_{ml} \sqrt{\frac{1}{2} + \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}} = \sqrt{2} V_L \times 0.9558 = 1.655 V_{ph}$$

### 4. Rectifier Mode vs Inverter Mode - For $0 \le \alpha < 90^\circ$: $\cos\alpha > 0 \implies V_{dc} > 0$. Real power flows from AC line to DC load (motoring). - At $\alpha = 90^\circ$: $V_{dc} = 0$. - For $90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$: $\cos\alpha < 0 \implies V_{dc} < 0$. With reversed DC source EMF ($E > |V_{dc}|$), the converter operates as a line-commutated inverter, feeding active power from DC side into the 3-phase grid.

Derivation & Problem Solving ID: PE-Q10

Explain the effect of AC source inductance on the performance of single-phase and three-phase full converters. Derive expressions for overlap angle (mu) and average voltage drop. Explain the effect of AC source inductance on the performance of single-phase and three-phase full converters. Derive expressions for overlap angle (mu) and average voltage drop. Explain the effect of AC source inductance on the performance of single-phase and three-phase full converters. Derive expressions for overlap angle (mu) and average voltage drop.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{V_m}, \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{1-ph}), \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{3-ph})$$

Source inductance $L_s$ prevents instantaneous current transfer, causing commutation overlap angle $\mu$. Output voltage drops by $\Delta V_d$. In inverter mode, if $\alpha + \mu > 180^\circ - \omega t_q$, commutation failure occurs.

$$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{V_m}, \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{1-ph}), \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{3-ph})$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{V_m}, \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{1-ph}), \quad \Delta V_d = \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} \ (\text{3-ph})$$

Source inductance $L_s$ prevents instantaneous current transfer, causing commutation overlap angle $\mu$. Output voltage drops by $\Delta V_d$. In inverter mode, if $\alpha + \mu > 180^\circ - \omega t_q$, commutation failure occurs.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In ideal rectifier analysis, the AC supply is assumed to be an ideal voltage source with zero internal impedance. However, in physical systems, utility distribution lines and transformer windings possess significant leakage inductance, termed **Source Inductance ($L_s$)**.

### 1. Commutation Overlap Phenomenon Because the current through an inductor cannot change discontinuously ($di/dt \ne \infty$), the current transfer from the outgoing thyristor pair to the incoming thyristor pair cannot happen instantaneously. - **Commutation Overlap Interval ($\mu$):** The finite angular duration during which both outgoing and incoming thyristors conduct simultaneously. - During overlap: 1. The AC source is momentarily short-circuited through the conducting thyristors. 2. The output terminal voltage drops to zero (in 1-phase bridge) or the mean of the commutating phase voltages (in 3-phase bridge). 3. The average DC terminal voltage is reduced by an overlap voltage drop $\Delta V_d$.

``` AC Source (v_s) ──[ L_s ]──┬────► Outgoing SCR (Current decaying from I_dc to 0) │ └────► Incoming SCR (Current rising from 0 to I_dc) Over interval: alpha to alpha + mu ```

### 2. Derivation of Overlap Angle ($\mu$) and Voltage Drop for 1-Phase Full Converter Consider the commutation from thyristors $T_1, T_2$ to $T_3, T_4$ occurring at $\omega t = \alpha$. During the overlap interval ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \alpha + \mu$): - All four thyristors ($T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$) are simultaneously conducting. - The output terminal voltage is shorted: $v_o(t) = 0$. - The source current $i_s(\omega t)$ transitions from $-I_{dc}$ to $+I_{dc}$.

Applying KVL to the AC loop: $$v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t = L_s \frac{di_s}{dt} = \omega L_s \frac{di_s}{d(\omega t)}$$

Rearranging and integrating from $\omega t = \alpha$ ($i_s = -I_{dc}$) to $\omega t = \alpha + \mu$ ($i_s = +I_{dc}$): $$\int_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \omega L_s \int_{-I_{dc}}^{+I_{dc}} di_s$$ $$V_m [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} = \omega L_s [I_{dc} - (-I_{dc})]$$ $$V_m [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)] = 2 \omega L_s I_{dc}$$

Solving for $\cos(\alpha + \mu)$: $$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{V_m}$$

The reduction in average DC output voltage due to the loss of volt-second area during overlap is: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$ Substituting $V_m [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)] = 2 \omega L_s I_{dc}$: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = 4 f L_s I_{dc}$$

The net average DC output voltage is: $$V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \Delta V_d = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc}$$ Alternatively written as: $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha + \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$

### 3. Extension to Three-Phase Fully Controlled Converter In a 3-phase full bridge converter, commutation occurs between thyristors in the same group every $60^\circ$ ($6$ commutations per supply cycle): $$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{\sqrt{3} V_{ml}}$$ where $V_{ml} = \sqrt{2} V_L$ is the peak line-to-line voltage.

Average voltage drop per commutation is $\frac{1}{\pi/3}\int \dots$, yielding: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = 6 f L_s I_{dc}$$

The net average DC output voltage of the 3-phase converter becomes: $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{2\pi}[\cos\alpha + \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$

### 4. Commutation Failure in Inverter Mode When operating in line-commutated inverter mode ($90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$), the extinction angle is defined as: $$\gamma = 180^\circ - (\alpha + \mu)$$ To prevent commutation failure, the circuit turn-off time $t_c = \gamma / \omega$ must be strictly greater than the device turn-off time $t_q$ ($t_c > t_q \implies \gamma > \omega t_q$). If load current $I_{dc}$ increases or line voltage dips, overlap angle $\mu$ increases. If $\alpha + \mu$ approaches $180^\circ$, the thyristor is subjected to forward voltage before inner junction $J_2$ has recovered, resulting in catastrophic **inverter commutation failure** and AC short circuit.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

In ideal rectifier analysis, the AC supply is assumed to be an ideal voltage source with zero internal impedance. However, in physical systems, utility distribution lines and transformer windings possess significant leakage inductance, termed **Source Inductance ($L_s$)**.

### 1. Commutation Overlap Phenomenon Because the current through an inductor cannot change discontinuously ($di/dt \ne \infty$), the current transfer from the outgoing thyristor pair to the incoming thyristor pair cannot happen instantaneously. - **Commutation Overlap Interval ($\mu$):** The finite angular duration during which both outgoing and incoming thyristors conduct simultaneously. - During overlap: 1. The AC source is momentarily short-circuited through the conducting thyristors. 2. The output terminal voltage drops to zero (in 1-phase bridge) or the mean of the commutating phase voltages (in 3-phase bridge). 3. The average DC terminal voltage is reduced by an overlap voltage drop $\Delta V_d$.

``` AC Source (v_s) ──[ L_s ]──┬────► Outgoing SCR (Current decaying from I_dc to 0) │ └────► Incoming SCR (Current rising from 0 to I_dc) Over interval: alpha to alpha + mu ```

### 2. Derivation of Overlap Angle ($\mu$) and Voltage Drop for 1-Phase Full Converter Consider the commutation from thyristors $T_1, T_2$ to $T_3, T_4$ occurring at $\omega t = \alpha$. During the overlap interval ($\alpha \le \omega t \le \alpha + \mu$): - All four thyristors ($T_1, T_2, T_3, T_4$) are simultaneously conducting. - The output terminal voltage is shorted: $v_o(t) = 0$. - The source current $i_s(\omega t)$ transitions from $-I_{dc}$ to $+I_{dc}$.

Applying KVL to the AC loop: $$v_s(t) = V_m \sin\omega t = L_s \frac{di_s}{dt} = \omega L_s \frac{di_s}{d(\omega t)}$$

Rearranging and integrating from $\omega t = \alpha$ ($i_s = -I_{dc}$) to $\omega t = \alpha + \mu$ ($i_s = +I_{dc}$): $$\int_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \omega L_s \int_{-I_{dc}}^{+I_{dc}} di_s$$ $$V_m [-\cos\omega t]_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} = \omega L_s [I_{dc} - (-I_{dc})]$$ $$V_m [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)] = 2 \omega L_s I_{dc}$$

Solving for $\cos(\alpha + \mu)$: $$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{V_m}$$

The reduction in average DC output voltage due to the loss of volt-second area during overlap is: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{1}{\pi} \int_\alpha^{\alpha + \mu} V_m \sin\omega t\,d(\omega t) = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$ Substituting $V_m [\cos\alpha - \cos(\alpha + \mu)] = 2 \omega L_s I_{dc}$: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = 4 f L_s I_{dc}$$

The net average DC output voltage is: $$V_{dc} = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \Delta V_d = \frac{2 V_m}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc}$$ Alternatively written as: $$V_{dc} = \frac{V_m}{\pi} [\cos\alpha + \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$

### 3. Extension to Three-Phase Fully Controlled Converter In a 3-phase full bridge converter, commutation occurs between thyristors in the same group every $60^\circ$ ($6$ commutations per supply cycle): $$\cos(\alpha + \mu) = \cos\alpha - \frac{2 \omega L_s I_{dc}}{\sqrt{3} V_{ml}}$$ where $V_{ml} = \sqrt{2} V_L$ is the peak line-to-line voltage.

Average voltage drop per commutation is $\frac{1}{\pi/3}\int \dots$, yielding: $$\Delta V_d = \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = 6 f L_s I_{dc}$$

The net average DC output voltage of the 3-phase converter becomes: $$V_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{\pi}\cos\alpha - \frac{3 \omega L_s}{\pi} I_{dc} = \frac{3 V_{ml}}{2\pi}[\cos\alpha + \cos(\alpha + \mu)]$$

### 4. Commutation Failure in Inverter Mode When operating in line-commutated inverter mode ($90^\circ < \alpha < 180^\circ$), the extinction angle is defined as: $$\gamma = 180^\circ - (\alpha + \mu)$$ To prevent commutation failure, the circuit turn-off time $t_c = \gamma / \omega$ must be strictly greater than the device turn-off time $t_q$ ($t_c > t_q \implies \gamma > \omega t_q$). If load current $I_{dc}$ increases or line voltage dips, overlap angle $\mu$ increases. If $\alpha + \mu$ approaches $180^\circ$, the thyristor is subjected to forward voltage before inner junction $J_2$ has recovered, resulting in catastrophic **inverter commutation failure** and AC short circuit.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_o = D V_s, \quad \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{f_s L}, \quad \Delta V_c = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{8 f_s^2 L C}, \quad L_c = \frac{(1 - D) R}{2 f_s}$$

Step-down (Buck) converter in CCM. Inductor volt-second balance yields linear gain $M = D$. Second-order LC filter delivers low output voltage ripple. Critical inductance $L_c$ guarantees continuous conduction at specified load resistance.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The step-down (Buck) converter produces a regulated DC output voltage lower than its DC input voltage ($V_o \le V_s$). It consists of a controlled semiconductor switch $S$ (MOSFET or IGBT), a freewheeling diode $D$, a low-pass filter inductor $L$, and a filter capacitor $C$ feeding a load resistance $R$.

``` + ───o───[ Switch S ]───┬───────[ Inductor L ]───┬────────o + │ │ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ D │ Diode │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o──────────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────o - ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation In continuous conduction mode, the inductor current $i_L(t)$ flows continuously without reaching zero at any instant during the switching period $T_s = 1/f_s$.

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** The switch conducts for duration $t_{on} = D T_s$, where $D = t_{on}/T_s$ is the duty ratio ($0 \le D \le 1$). Diode $D$ is reverse-biased with cathode at $V_s$. The voltage across inductor $L$ is: $$v_L(t) = V_s - V_o$$ The inductor current rises linearly from its minimum value $I_1$ to its maximum value $I_2$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s - V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = I_2 - I_1 = \frac{V_s - V_o}{L} D T_s$$

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** The switch is opened for duration $t_{off} = (1 - D)T_s$. The collapsing magnetic field in $L$ forces diode $D$ into forward conduction (freewheeling action). The voltage across inductor $L$ is clamped to: $$v_L(t) = -V_o$$ The inductor current decays linearly from $I_2$ back to $I_1$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_o}{L}(1 - D)T_s$$

### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio In periodic steady-state operation, the net change in inductor current over a complete switching cycle must be identically zero: $$\Delta i_L = \frac{1}{L}\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0 \implies \int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

Substituting the piecewise inductor voltages: $$(V_s - V_o) D T_s + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$(V_s - V_o) D - V_o(1 - D) = 0$$ $$V_s D - V_o D - V_o + V_o D = 0 \implies V_o = D V_s$$

The DC voltage conversion ratio is: $$M = \frac{V_o}{V_s} = D$$ Since $0 < D < 1$, the output voltage is always less than the input voltage ($V_o < V_s$).

### 3. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) Substituting $V_o = D V_s$ and $T_s = 1/f_s$: $$\Delta I_L = \frac{(V_s - V_o) D}{f_s L} = \frac{V_s(1 - D) D}{f_s L}$$ The maximum ripple current occurs at a duty ratio of $D = 0.5$: $$\Delta I_{L,\max} = \frac{V_s}{4 f_s L}$$

### 4. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) The capacitor current is $i_c(t) = i_L(t) - I_o$. Since the average capacitor current in steady state is zero, the DC load current is supplied entirely by the average inductor current ($I_L = I_o$). The AC triangular ripple current flows through the filter capacitor. The total charge $\Delta Q$ accumulated on the capacitor during the positive half of the triangular ripple cycle is the area of the upper triangular region (base $T_s / 2$ and height $\Delta I_L / 2$): $$\Delta Q = \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{T_s}{2}\right) \left(\frac{\Delta I_L}{2}\right) = \frac{T_s \Delta I_L}{8} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s}$$

The resulting output ripple voltage is: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s C} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$ Expressing as a normalized voltage ripple fraction: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$

### 5. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) and Critical Capacitance ($C_c$) At the boundary between Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) and Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM), the minimum inductor current just touches zero ($I_{1} = 0$): $$I_{L\min} = I_L - \frac{\Delta I_L}{2} = 0 \implies I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Since $I_L = I_o = \frac{V_o}{R} = \frac{D V_s}{R}$: $$\frac{D V_s}{R} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Solving for the critical inductance $L_c$: $$L_c = \frac{(1 - D) R}{2 f_s}$$ To ensure continuous conduction under all duty cycle conditions down to minimum load, the filter inductance must satisfy: $$L \ge L_{c,\max} = \frac{R_{\max}}{2 f_s} \quad (\text{as } D \to 0)$$

The critical filter capacitance required to maintain ripple voltage below a target specification $\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}$ is: $$C_c = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L \left(\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}\right)}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The step-down (Buck) converter produces a regulated DC output voltage lower than its DC input voltage ($V_o \le V_s$). It consists of a controlled semiconductor switch $S$ (MOSFET or IGBT), a freewheeling diode $D$, a low-pass filter inductor $L$, and a filter capacitor $C$ feeding a load resistance $R$.

``` + ───o───[ Switch S ]───┬───────[ Inductor L ]───┬────────o + │ │ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ D │ Diode │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o──────────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────o - ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation In continuous conduction mode, the inductor current $i_L(t)$ flows continuously without reaching zero at any instant during the switching period $T_s = 1/f_s$.

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** The switch conducts for duration $t_{on} = D T_s$, where $D = t_{on}/T_s$ is the duty ratio ($0 \le D \le 1$). - **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** The switch is opened for duration $t_{off} = (1 - D)T_s$. ### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio In periodic steady-state operation, the net change in inductor current over a complete switching cycle must be identically zero: $$\Delta i_L = \frac{1}{L}\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0 \implies \int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

The DC voltage conversion ratio is: $$M = \frac{V_o}{V_s} = D$$ Since $0 < D < 1$, the output voltage is always less than the input voltage ($V_o < V_s$).

### 3. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) Substituting $V_o = D V_s$ and $T_s = 1/f_s$: $$\Delta I_L = \frac{(V_s - V_o) D}{f_s L} = \frac{V_s(1 - D) D}{f_s L}$$ The maximum ripple current occurs at a duty ratio of $D = 0.5$: $$\Delta I_{L,\max} = \frac{V_s}{4 f_s L}$$

### 4. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) The capacitor current is $i_c(t) = i_L(t) - I_o$. Since the average capacitor current in steady state is zero, the DC load current is supplied entirely by the average inductor current ($I_L = I_o$). The AC triangular ripple current flows through the filter capacitor. The total charge $\Delta Q$ accumulated on the capacitor during the positive half of the triangular ripple cycle is the area of the upper triangular region (base $T_s / 2$ and height $\Delta I_L / 2$): $$\Delta Q = \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{T_s}{2}\right) \left(\frac{\Delta I_L}{2}\right) = \frac{T_s \Delta I_L}{8} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s}$$

The resulting output ripple voltage is: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s C} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$ Expressing as a normalized voltage ripple fraction: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$

### 5. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) and Critical Capacitance ($C_c$) At the boundary between Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) and Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM), the minimum inductor current just touches zero ($I_{1} = 0$): $$I_{L\min} = I_L - \frac{\Delta I_L}{2} = 0 \implies I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Since $I_L = I_o = \frac{V_o}{R} = \frac{D V_s}{R}$: $$\frac{D V_s}{R} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Solving for the critical inductance $L_c$: $$L_c = \frac{(1 - D) R}{2 f_s}$$ To ensure continuous conduction under all duty cycle conditions down to minimum load, the filter inductance must satisfy: $$L \ge L_{c,\max} = \frac{R_{\max}}{2 f_s} \quad (\text{as } D \to 0)$$

The critical filter capacitance required to maintain ripple voltage below a target specification $\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}$ is: $$C_c = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L \left(\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}\right)}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The step-down (Buck) converter produces a regulated DC output voltage lower than its DC input voltage ($V_o \le V_s$). It consists of a controlled semiconductor switch $S$ (MOSFET or IGBT), a freewheeling diode $D$, a low-pass filter inductor $L$, and a filter capacitor $C$ feeding a load resistance $R$.

``` + ───o───[ Switch S ]───┬───────[ Inductor L ]───┬────────o + │ │ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ D │ Diode │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o──────────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────o - ```

### 1. Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) Operation In continuous conduction mode, the inductor current $i_L(t)$ flows continuously without reaching zero at any instant during the switching period $T_s = 1/f_s$.

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** The switch conducts for duration $t_{on} = D T_s$, where $D = t_{on}/T_s$ is the duty ratio ($0 \le D \le 1$). Diode $D$ is reverse-biased with cathode at $V_s$. The voltage across inductor $L$ is: $$v_L(t) = V_s - V_o$$ The inductor current rises linearly from its minimum value $I_1$ to its maximum value $I_2$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s - V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = I_2 - I_1 = \frac{V_s - V_o}{L} D T_s$$

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** The switch is opened for duration $t_{off} = (1 - D)T_s$. The collapsing magnetic field in $L$ forces diode $D$ into forward conduction (freewheeling action). The voltage across inductor $L$ is clamped to: $$v_L(t) = -V_o$$ The inductor current decays linearly from $I_2$ back to $I_1$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_o}{L}(1 - D)T_s$$

### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio In periodic steady-state operation, the net change in inductor current over a complete switching cycle must be identically zero: $$\Delta i_L = \frac{1}{L}\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0 \implies \int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

Substituting the piecewise inductor voltages: $$(V_s - V_o) D T_s + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$(V_s - V_o) D - V_o(1 - D) = 0$$ $$V_s D - V_o D - V_o + V_o D = 0 \implies V_o = D V_s$$

The DC voltage conversion ratio is: $$M = \frac{V_o}{V_s} = D$$ Since $0 < D < 1$, the output voltage is always less than the input voltage ($V_o < V_s$).

### 3. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) Substituting $V_o = D V_s$ and $T_s = 1/f_s$: $$\Delta I_L = \frac{(V_s - V_o) D}{f_s L} = \frac{V_s(1 - D) D}{f_s L}$$ The maximum ripple current occurs at a duty ratio of $D = 0.5$: $$\Delta I_{L,\max} = \frac{V_s}{4 f_s L}$$

### 4. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) The capacitor current is $i_c(t) = i_L(t) - I_o$. Since the average capacitor current in steady state is zero, the DC load current is supplied entirely by the average inductor current ($I_L = I_o$). The AC triangular ripple current flows through the filter capacitor. The total charge $\Delta Q$ accumulated on the capacitor during the positive half of the triangular ripple cycle is the area of the upper triangular region (base $T_s / 2$ and height $\Delta I_L / 2$): $$\Delta Q = \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{T_s}{2}\right) \left(\frac{\Delta I_L}{2}\right) = \frac{T_s \Delta I_L}{8} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s}$$

The resulting output ripple voltage is: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{\Delta I_L}{8 f_s C} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$ Expressing as a normalized voltage ripple fraction: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L C}$$

### 5. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) and Critical Capacitance ($C_c$) At the boundary between Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) and Discontinuous Conduction Mode (DCM), the minimum inductor current just touches zero ($I_{1} = 0$): $$I_{L\min} = I_L - \frac{\Delta I_L}{2} = 0 \implies I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Since $I_L = I_o = \frac{V_o}{R} = \frac{D V_s}{R}$: $$\frac{D V_s}{R} = \frac{V_s D(1 - D)}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Solving for the critical inductance $L_c$: $$L_c = \frac{(1 - D) R}{2 f_s}$$ To ensure continuous conduction under all duty cycle conditions down to minimum load, the filter inductance must satisfy: $$L \ge L_{c,\max} = \frac{R_{\max}}{2 f_s} \quad (\text{as } D \to 0)$$

The critical filter capacitance required to maintain ripple voltage below a target specification $\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}$ is: $$C_c = \frac{1 - D}{8 f_s^2 L \left(\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o}\right)}$$

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 3.1: Step-Down (Buck) Converter Topology, Equivalent Sub-Circuits, and Steady-State Waveforms — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Derivation & Analytical ID: PE-Q12

Explain the operation of a step-up (Boost) converter in continuous conduction mode. Derive the voltage gain using inductor volt-second balance, and calculate the critical inductance for CCM. Explain the operation of a step-up (Boost) converter in continuous conduction mode. Derive the voltage gain using inductor volt-second balance, and calculate the critical inductance for CCM. Explain the operation of a step-up (Boost) converter in continuous conduction mode. Derive the voltage gain using inductor volt-second balance, and calculate the critical inductance for CCM.

$$V_o = \frac{V_s}{1 - D}, \quad \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L}, \quad \Delta V_c = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C}, \quad L_c = \frac{D(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_o = \frac{V_s}{1 - D}, \quad \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L}, \quad \Delta V_c = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C}, \quad L_c = \frac{D(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

Step-up (Boost) converter in CCM. Energy stored during switch ON adds in series with DC source during switch OFF. Output capacitor supplies load while switch is ON. Inductor parasitic resistance limits maximum practical voltage gain.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The step-up (Boost) converter converts a lower DC input voltage $V_s$ to a higher stabilized DC output voltage $V_o$ ($V_o \ge V_s$). It is widely utilized in photovoltaic maximum power point trackers, power factor correction (PFC) pre-regulators, and electric vehicle battery boost stages.

``` + ───o───[ Inductor L ]───┬───►| Diode D ───┬────────o + │ │ ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ Switch S │ │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─────┬─────┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o────────────────────┴─────────────────┴────────o - ```

### 1. Operating Modes in Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM) - **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** Switch $S$ is closed for duration $D T_s$. The diode $D$ is reverse-biased because its cathode is held at output voltage $V_o$ while anode is at ground potential ($v_D = -V_o$). The inductor is placed directly across the DC source: $$v_L(t) = V_s$$ Inductor current increases linearly from $I_1$ to $I_2$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s}{L} D T_s$$ During this interval, the load resistance $R$ is isolated from the source and is powered solely by the discharging output capacitor $C$.

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** Switch $S$ is opened for duration $(1 - D)T_s$. The collapsing magnetic field induces a positive voltage across the inductor ($v_L = L \frac{di}{dt}$), which adds in series with the input voltage, forward-biasing diode $D$. The inductor voltage is: $$v_L(t) = V_s - V_o$$ Since $V_o > V_s$, $v_L(t)$ is negative, and the inductor current decreases linearly from $I_2$ to $I_1$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s - V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_o - V_s}{L}(1 - D)T_s$$ During this mode, energy is transferred simultaneously from both the DC source and the discharging inductor into capacitor $C$ and load $R$.

### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio Under steady-state periodic operation, the average voltage across the inductor over one full switching period must be zero: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

$$V_s (D T_s) + (V_s - V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$V_s D + V_s - V_o - V_s D + V_o D = 0$$ $$V_s - V_o(1 - D) = 0 \implies V_o = \frac{V_s}{1 - D}$$

The voltage gain is: $$M = \frac{V_o}{V_s} = \frac{1}{1 - D}$$ Since $0 \le D < 1$, the denominator $(1 - D) \le 1$, meaning $V_o \ge V_s$ under all operating conditions (Step-Up Converter).

### 3. Ripple Formulations

#### A. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) $$\Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D T_s}{L} = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L}$$

#### B. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) Unlike the buck converter where inductor current directly feeds the capacitor, in a boost converter the diode current is discontinuous (pulsating). During Mode 1 ($0 < t \le D T_s$), the diode is off and the capacitor must supply the entire continuous DC load current $I_o = V_o / R$. The total charge lost by the capacitor during this switch-on interval is: $$\Delta Q = I_o \cdot t_{on} = I_o (D T_s) = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s}$$

The peak-to-peak output voltage ripple is therefore: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C}$$ Expressed as a fractional ripple: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{D}{R f_s C}$$

### 4. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) for Continuous Conduction At the boundary between CCM and DCM: $$I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Assuming an ideal lossless converter ($P_{in} = P_{out} \implies V_s I_L = V_o I_o$): $$I_L = \frac{V_o}{V_s} I_o = \frac{1}{1 - D} I_o = \frac{V_o}{R(1 - D)} = \frac{V_s}{R(1 - D)^2}$$

Equating average inductor current to half the ripple current: $$\frac{V_s}{R(1 - D)^2} = \frac{V_s D}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Solving for critical inductance $L_c$: $$L_c = \frac{D(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

To find the worst-case maximum value of $L_c$, differentiate with respect to $D$: $$\frac{d}{dD}\left[ D(1 - D)^2 \right] = (1 - D)^2 - 2D(1 - D) = (1 - D)(1 - 3D) = 0 \implies D = \frac{1}{3}$$ $$L_{c,\max} = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2 R}{2 f_s} = \frac{4 R}{54 f_s} = \frac{2 R}{27 f_s}$$

### 5. Effect of Parasitic Internal Resistance on Maximum Practical Gain In a real inductor with winding resistance $r_L$, the actual voltage transfer ratio is: $$\frac{V_o}{V_s} = \frac{1}{1 - D} \left[ \frac{1}{1 + \frac{r_L}{R(1 - D)^2}} \right]$$ As $D \to 1$, the term $\frac{r_L}{R(1 - D)^2}$ grows astronomically, causing the actual gain to plummet toward zero instead of rising toward infinity. Practical boost converters are limited to duty cycles $D \le 0.85$ and voltage gains of $M \le 4\text{ to }5$.

#### B. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) Unlike the buck converter where inductor current directly feeds the capacitor, in a boost converter the diode current is discontinuous (pulsating). The peak-to-peak output voltage ripple is therefore: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C}$$ Expressed as a fractional ripple: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{D}{R f_s C}$$

### 4. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) for Continuous Conduction At the boundary between CCM and DCM: $$I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Assuming an ideal lossless converter ($P_{in} = P_{out} \implies V_s I_L = V_o I_o$): $$I_L = \frac{V_o}{V_s} I_o = \frac{1}{1 - D} I_o = \frac{V_o}{R(1 - D)} = \frac{V_s}{R(1 - D)^2}$$

Equating average inductor current to half the ripple current: $$\frac{V_s}{R(1 - D)^2} = \frac{V_s D}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Solving for critical inductance $L_c$: $$L_c = \frac{D(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

To find the worst-case maximum value of $L_c$, differentiate with respect to $D$: $$\frac{d}{dD}\left[ D(1 - D)^2 \right] = (1 - D)^2 - 2D(1 - D) = (1 - D)(1 - 3D) = 0 \implies D = \frac{1}{3}$$ $$L_{c,\max} = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2 R}{2 f_s} = \frac{4 R}{54 f_s} = \frac{2 R}{27 f_s}$$

### 5. Effect of Parasitic Internal Resistance on Maximum Practical Gain In a real inductor with winding resistance $r_L$, the actual voltage transfer ratio is: $$\frac{V_o}{V_s} = \frac{1}{1 - D} \left[ \frac{1}{1 + \frac{r_L}{R(1 - D)^2}} \right]$$ As $D \to 1$, the term $\frac{r_L}{R(1 - D)^2}$ grows astronomically, causing the actual gain to plummet toward zero instead of rising toward infinity. Practical boost converters are limited to duty cycles $D \le 0.85$ and voltage gains of $M \le 4\text{ to }5$.

``` + ───o───[ Inductor L ]───┬───►| Diode D ───┬────────o + │ │ ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ Switch S │ │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─────┬─────┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o────────────────────┴─────────────────┴────────o - ```

#### B. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) Unlike the buck converter where inductor current directly feeds the capacitor, in a boost converter the diode current is discontinuous (pulsating). During Mode 1 ($0 < t \le D T_s$), the diode is off and the capacitor must supply the entire continuous DC load current $I_o = V_o / R$. The peak-to-peak output voltage ripple is therefore: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C}$$ Expressed as a fractional ripple: $$\frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{D}{R f_s C}$$

### 4. Critical Inductance ($L_c$) for Continuous Conduction At the boundary between CCM and DCM: $$I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

Assuming an ideal lossless converter ($P_{in} = P_{out} \implies V_s I_L = V_o I_o$): $$I_L = \frac{V_o}{V_s} I_o = \frac{1}{1 - D} I_o = \frac{V_o}{R(1 - D)} = \frac{V_s}{R(1 - D)^2}$$

Practical boost converters are limited to duty cycles $D \le 0.85$ and voltage gains of $M \le 4\text{ to }5$.

Derive the voltage transfer ratio of an inverting Buck-Boost converter in continuous conduction mode. Derive expressions for inductor ripple current and critical boundary inductance. Inverting Buck-Boost converter provides step-down ($D < 0.5$) and step-up ($D > 0.5$) voltage translation with reversed output polarity. Inductor acts as intermediate energy reservoir between input and output ports.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Buck-Boost converter is a DC-DC converter topology capable of producing a regulated DC output voltage whose magnitude can be either greater than, equal to, or less than the DC input voltage magnitude. The output voltage exhibits an inherent polarity inversion relative to the input.

``` + ───o───[ Switch S ]───┬───|◄── Diode D ───┬────────o - │ │ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ L │ Inductor │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o──────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────o + ```

### 1. Operational Modes in Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM)

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** Switch $S$ is closed. The diode $D$ is reverse-biased because its cathode is held at $+V_s$ while its anode is at the negative load potential ($v_D = -(V_s + |V_o|)$). The input source is connected directly across inductor $L$: $$v_L(t) = V_s$$ Inductor current increases linearly from $I_1$ to $I_2$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s}{L} D T_s$$ Energy is absorbed from the DC source and stored in the magnetic field of inductor $L$. The load is isolated and sustained entirely by capacitor $C$.

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** Switch $S$ is opened. The inductor current cannot stop instantaneously. The collapsing magnetic field reverses the polarity of the voltage induced across inductor $L$ (terminal at diode becomes positive relative to ground), forward-biasing diode $D$. The inductor discharges its stored energy directly into the parallel combination of capacitor $C$ and load $R$: $$v_L(t) = -V_o$$ Inductor current decays linearly from $I_2$ back to $I_1$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_o}{L}(1 - D)T_s$$

### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio Applying Faraday's law of induction over one complete switching period in steady state: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

$$V_s (D T_s) + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$V_s D - V_o(1 - D) = 0 \implies V_o = \frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

Accounting for the opposite reference polarity (negative with respect to common ground): $$V_o = -\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

- **Operational Regions:** 1. **Buck Region ($0 < D < 0.5$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} < 1 \implies |V_o| < V_s$$ 2. **Boost Region ($0.5 < D < 1$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} > 1 \implies |V_o| > V_s$$ 3. **Unity Gain ($D = 0.5$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} = 1 \implies |V_o| = V_s$$

### 3. Ripple Current and Ripple Voltage Formulations

#### A. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) $$\Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L}$$

#### B. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) During Mode 1 ($0 < t \le D T_s$), the diode is reverse-biased, and capacitor $C$ supplies the entire load current $I_o = V_o / R$. The total charge extracted from the capacitor is: $$\Delta Q = I_o (D T_s) = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s}$$ The resulting output voltage ripple is: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C} \implies \frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{D}{R f_s C}$$

### 4. Critical Boundary Inductance ($L_c$) for CCM At the CCM/DCM boundary condition: $$I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

From power balance ($P_{in} = P_{out} \implies V_s I_{in} = V_o I_o$): The average input current is $I_{in} = D I_L$, and average diode current is $I_D = (1 - D)I_L = I_o$. Therefore, the average inductor current is: $$I_L = \frac{I_o}{1 - D} = \frac{V_o}{R(1 - D)}$$

Substituting $V_o = \frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$: $$I_L = \frac{\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s}{R(1 - D)} = \frac{D V_s}{R(1 - D)^2}$$

Equating to half the ripple current: $$\frac{D V_s}{R(1 - D)^2} = \frac{V_s D}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Cancelling $D V_s$ from both sides: $$L_c = \frac{(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

To ensure CCM across all operating duty cycles up to $D \to 0$: $$L \ge L_{c,\max} = \frac{R_{\max}}{2 f_s}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Buck-Boost converter is a DC-DC converter topology capable of producing a regulated DC output voltage whose magnitude can be either greater than, equal to, or less than the DC input voltage magnitude. The output voltage exhibits an inherent polarity inversion relative to the input.

``` + ───o───[ Switch S ]───┬───|◄── Diode D ───┬────────o - │ │ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ L │ Inductor │ C │ [R] Load V_o └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ - ───o──────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────o + ```

### 1. Operational Modes in Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM)

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** Switch $S$ is closed. The diode $D$ is reverse-biased because its cathode is held at $+V_s$ while its anode is at the negative load potential ($v_D = -(V_s + |V_o|)$). The input source is connected directly across inductor $L$: $$v_L(t) = V_s$$ Inductor current increases linearly from $I_1$ to $I_2$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_s}{L} D T_s$$ Energy is absorbed from the DC source and stored in the magnetic field of inductor $L$. The load is isolated and sustained entirely by capacitor $C$.

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** Switch $S$ is opened. The inductor current cannot stop instantaneously. The collapsing magnetic field reverses the polarity of the voltage induced across inductor $L$ (terminal at diode becomes positive relative to ground), forward-biasing diode $D$. The inductor discharges its stored energy directly into the parallel combination of capacitor $C$ and load $R$: $$v_L(t) = -V_o$$ Inductor current decays linearly from $I_2$ back to $I_1$: $$\frac{di_L}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L} \implies \Delta I_L = \frac{V_o}{L}(1 - D)T_s$$

### 2. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Voltage Transfer Ratio Applying Faraday's law of induction over one complete switching period in steady state: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_L(t)\,dt = 0$$

$$V_s (D T_s) + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$V_s D - V_o(1 - D) = 0 \implies V_o = \frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

Accounting for the opposite reference polarity (negative with respect to common ground): $$V_o = -\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

- **Operational Regions:** 1. **Buck Region ($0 < D < 0.5$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} < 1 \implies |V_o| < V_s$$ 2. **Boost Region ($0.5 < D < 1$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} > 1 \implies |V_o| > V_s$$ 3. **Unity Gain ($D = 0.5$):** $$\frac{D}{1 - D} = 1 \implies |V_o| = V_s$$

### 3. Ripple Current and Ripple Voltage Formulations

#### A. Inductor Peak-to-Peak Ripple Current ($\Delta I_L$) $$\Delta I_L = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L}$$

#### B. Output Peak-to-Peak Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_c$) During Mode 1 ($0 < t \le D T_s$), the diode is reverse-biased, and capacitor $C$ supplies the entire load current $I_o = V_o / R$. The total charge extracted from the capacitor is: $$\Delta Q = I_o (D T_s) = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s}$$ The resulting output voltage ripple is: $$\Delta V_c = \frac{\Delta Q}{C} = \frac{V_o D}{R f_s C} \implies \frac{\Delta V_o}{V_o} = \frac{D}{R f_s C}$$

### 4. Critical Boundary Inductance ($L_c$) for CCM At the CCM/DCM boundary condition: $$I_L = \frac{\Delta I_L}{2}$$

From power balance ($P_{in} = P_{out} \implies V_s I_{in} = V_o I_o$): The average input current is $I_{in} = D I_L$, and average diode current is $I_D = (1 - D)I_L = I_o$. Therefore, the average inductor current is: $$I_L = \frac{I_o}{1 - D} = \frac{V_o}{R(1 - D)}$$

Substituting $V_o = \frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$: $$I_L = \frac{\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s}{R(1 - D)} = \frac{D V_s}{R(1 - D)^2}$$

Equating to half the ripple current: $$\frac{D V_s}{R(1 - D)^2} = \frac{V_s D}{2 f_s L_c}$$ Cancelling $D V_s$ from both sides: $$L_c = \frac{(1 - D)^2 R}{2 f_s}$$

To ensure CCM across all operating duty cycles up to $D \to 0$: $$L \ge L_{c,\max} = \frac{R_{\max}}{2 f_s}$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Ćuk converter, developed by Slobodan Ćuk at Caltech, is an advanced DC-DC converter topology capable of both step-down and step-up voltage conversion with inverting output polarity.

Unlike the conventional Buck, Boost, and Buck-Boost converters that store and transfer energy inductively via magnetic flux in an inductor, the Ćuk converter utilizes an intermediate coupling capacitor ($C_1$) as the primary energy storage and transfer element.

``` + ──[ L_1 ]───┬───────[ C_1 ]───────┬───[ L_2 ]───┬────────o - │ │ │ ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ Switch S │ │ D │ Diode │ C_2│ [R] Load V_o └─────┬─────┘ └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ │ - ────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────┴────────o + ```

### 1. Outstanding Advantages over Conventional Converters 1. **Continuous Input Current:** Inductor $L_1$ is in series with the input DC source, preventing pulsating current spikes. 2. **Continuous Output Current:** Inductor $L_2$ is in series with the output load, resulting in virtually ripple-free output current. 3. **Low EMI Signature:** Pulsating currents are eliminated at both input and output terminals, drastically downsizing external electromagnetic interference (EMI) filtering requirements.

### 2. Operating Modes in Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM)

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** Switch $S$ is closed. The diode $D$ is reverse-biased by the positive charge on coupling capacitor $C_1$ ($v_D = -V_{C1}$). 1. Inductor $L_1$ is connected directly across the DC source: $$v_{L1}(t) = V_s \implies \frac{di_{L1}}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{L_1}$$ 2. Coupling capacitor $C_1$ discharges its energy into the output circuit through switch $S$, feeding inductor $L_2$, output capacitor $C_2$, and load $R$: $$v_{L2}(t) = -(V_{C1} + V_o) \implies \frac{di_{L2}}{dt} = -\frac{V_{C1} + V_o}{L_2}$$

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** Switch $S$ is opened. Inductor currents $i_{L1}$ and $i_{L2}$ force diode $D$ into forward conduction. 1. Inductor $L_1$ charges coupling capacitor $C_1$ through diode $D$: $$v_{L1}(t) = V_s - V_{C1} \implies \frac{di_{L1}}{dt} = \frac{V_s - V_{C1}}{L_1}$$ 2. Inductor $L_2$ discharges its stored magnetic energy through diode $D$ into the output stage: $$v_{L2}(t) = -V_o \implies \frac{di_{L2}}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L_2}$$

### 3. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Derivation of Voltage Gain Applying volt-second balance across input inductor $L_1$: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_{L1}(t)\,dt = 0 \implies V_s (D T_s) + (V_s - V_{C1})(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$V_s D + V_s - V_{C1} - V_s D + V_{C1} D = 0 \implies V_{C1}(1 - D) = V_s$$ $$V_{C1} = \frac{V_s}{1 - D}$$

Applying volt-second balance across output inductor $L_2$: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_{L2}(t)\,dt = 0 \implies -(V_{C1} + V_o) D T_s + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$-V_{C1} D - V_o D - V_o + V_o D = 0 \implies V_o = -D V_{C1}$$

Substituting the expression for $V_{C1}$: $$V_o = -D \left(\frac{V_s}{1 - D}\right) = -\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

The magnitude relationship is identical to the Buck-Boost converter: - $|V_o| < V_s$ for $D < 0.5$ (Step-down) - $|V_o| > V_s$ for $D > 0.5$ (Step-up) - Output voltage is inverted in polarity.

### 4. Ripple Formulations - **Input Inductor Ripple Current ($\Delta I_{L1}$):** $$\Delta I_{L1} = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L_1}$$ - **Output Inductor Ripple Current ($\Delta I_{L2}$):** $$\Delta I_{L2} = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L_2}$$ - **Coupling Capacitor Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_{C1}$):** During switch OFF, capacitor $C_1$ is charged by current $I_{s}$: $$\Delta V_{C1} = \frac{I_s (1 - D) T_s}{C_1} = \frac{I_s (1 - D)}{f_s C_1} = \frac{I_o D}{f_s C_1}$$ - **Output Capacitor Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_{C2}$):** Since $L_2$ provides continuous current, output ripple voltage follows the second-order filter formulation of the buck converter: $$\Delta V_{C2} = \frac{\Delta I_{L2}}{8 f_s C_2} = \frac{V_s D}{8 f_s^2 L_2 C_2}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Ćuk converter, developed by Slobodan Ćuk at Caltech, is an advanced DC-DC converter topology capable of both step-down and step-up voltage conversion with inverting output polarity.

Unlike the conventional Buck, Boost, and Buck-Boost converters that store and transfer energy inductively via magnetic flux in an inductor, the Ćuk converter utilizes an intermediate coupling capacitor ($C_1$) as the primary energy storage and transfer element.

``` + ──[ L_1 ]───┬───────[ C_1 ]───────┬───[ L_2 ]───┬────────o - │ │ │ ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─┴─┐ ┌─┴─┐ │ Switch S │ │ D │ Diode │ C_2│ [R] Load V_o └─────┬─────┘ └─┬─┘ └─┬─┘ │ │ │ - ────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────┴────────o + ```

### 1. Outstanding Advantages over Conventional Converters 1. **Continuous Input Current:** Inductor $L_1$ is in series with the input DC source, preventing pulsating current spikes. 2. **Continuous Output Current:** Inductor $L_2$ is in series with the output load, resulting in virtually ripple-free output current. 3. **Low EMI Signature:** Pulsating currents are eliminated at both input and output terminals, drastically downsizing external electromagnetic interference (EMI) filtering requirements.

### 2. Operating Modes in Continuous Conduction Mode (CCM)

- **Mode 1: Switch $S$ is ON ($0 < t \le D T_s$):** Switch $S$ is closed. The diode $D$ is reverse-biased by the positive charge on coupling capacitor $C_1$ ($v_D = -V_{C1}$). 1. Inductor $L_1$ is connected directly across the DC source: $$v_{L1}(t) = V_s \implies \frac{di_{L1}}{dt} = \frac{V_s}{L_1}$$ 2. Coupling capacitor $C_1$ discharges its energy into the output circuit through switch $S$, feeding inductor $L_2$, output capacitor $C_2$, and load $R$: $$v_{L2}(t) = -(V_{C1} + V_o) \implies \frac{di_{L2}}{dt} = -\frac{V_{C1} + V_o}{L_2}$$

- **Mode 2: Switch $S$ is OFF ($D T_s < t \le T_s$):** Switch $S$ is opened. Inductor currents $i_{L1}$ and $i_{L2}$ force diode $D$ into forward conduction. 1. Inductor $L_1$ charges coupling capacitor $C_1$ through diode $D$: $$v_{L1}(t) = V_s - V_{C1} \implies \frac{di_{L1}}{dt} = \frac{V_s - V_{C1}}{L_1}$$ 2. Inductor $L_2$ discharges its stored magnetic energy through diode $D$ into the output stage: $$v_{L2}(t) = -V_o \implies \frac{di_{L2}}{dt} = -\frac{V_o}{L_2}$$

### 3. Inductor Volt-Second Balance and Derivation of Voltage Gain Applying volt-second balance across input inductor $L_1$: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_{L1}(t)\,dt = 0 \implies V_s (D T_s) + (V_s - V_{C1})(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$V_s D + V_s - V_{C1} - V_s D + V_{C1} D = 0 \implies V_{C1}(1 - D) = V_s$$ $$V_{C1} = \frac{V_s}{1 - D}$$

Applying volt-second balance across output inductor $L_2$: $$\int_0^{T_s} v_{L2}(t)\,dt = 0 \implies -(V_{C1} + V_o) D T_s + (-V_o)(1 - D) T_s = 0$$ $$-V_{C1} D - V_o D - V_o + V_o D = 0 \implies V_o = -D V_{C1}$$

Substituting the expression for $V_{C1}$: $$V_o = -D \left(\frac{V_s}{1 - D}\right) = -\frac{D}{1 - D} V_s$$

The magnitude relationship is identical to the Buck-Boost converter: - $|V_o| < V_s$ for $D < 0.5$ (Step-down) - $|V_o| > V_s$ for $D > 0.5$ (Step-up) - Output voltage is inverted in polarity.

### 4. Ripple Formulations - **Input Inductor Ripple Current ($\Delta I_{L1}$):** $$\Delta I_{L1} = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L_1}$$ - **Output Inductor Ripple Current ($\Delta I_{L2}$):** $$\Delta I_{L2} = \frac{V_s D}{f_s L_2}$$ - **Coupling Capacitor Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_{C1}$):** During switch OFF, capacitor $C_1$ is charged by current $I_{s}$: $$\Delta V_{C1} = \frac{I_s (1 - D) T_s}{C_1} = \frac{I_s (1 - D)}{f_s C_1} = \frac{I_o D}{f_s C_1}$$ - **Output Capacitor Ripple Voltage ($\Delta V_{C2}$):** Since $L_2$ provides continuous current, output ripple voltage follows the second-order filter formulation of the buck converter: $$\Delta V_{C2} = \frac{\Delta I_{L2}}{8 f_s C_2} = \frac{V_s D}{8 f_s^2 L_2 C_2}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി
